The state-of-the-art results achieved on GenEval and T2I CompBench demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed DiT-Air and DiT-Air-Lite architectures.

However, the paper has some limitations.  The improvements, while notable, are incremental.  The core architecture is still DiT. The novelty rests primarily on specific design choices and the thoroughness of the experimentation. A more in-depth analysis of the failure cases and limitations of DiT-Air and DiT-Air-Lite would further strengthen the paper. Additionally, the reliance on proprietary datasets and models (in-house VAE, CLIP-H, LLM) makes the results less directly reproducible by the broader research community and somewhat limits the impact. While the authors include a detailed breakdown of hyperparameters in the appendix, other crucial factors concerning in-house models and datasets remain undisclosed. Moreover, some ablations could be more detailed (e.g., the reward fine-tuning, full parameters about fine-tuning the LLM encoder).

The significance of the paper lies in its contribution to more efficient and effective DiT architectures for text-to-image generation.  The findings are valuable for researchers and practitioners looking to build high-performance text-to-image models with limited computational resources. Also, achieving superior results with simpler architectures will push researchers to focus on more efficient techniques.

Score: 7.5

**Justification:**

The score reflects the paper's contribution to the field of text-to-image generation, specifically in improving the efficiency of DiT architectures. The experimental evaluation is thorough and the results are strong. However, the novelty is somewhat incremental, building upon existing DiT frameworks. The reliance on proprietary components makes direct replication challenging, which impacts its immediate influence. Although the parameter sharing isn't entirely novel, it is cleverly implemented. As a result, the score of 7.5 captures a notable, rigorous, and well executed improvement of well-established approaches and achieves SOTA scores.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Siege: Autonomous Multi-Turn Jailbreaking of Large Language Models with Tree Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10619v1)
- **Authors**: Andy Zhou
- **Abstract**: We introduce Siege, a multi-turn adversarial framework that models the gradual erosion of Large Language Model (LLM) safety through a tree search perspective. Unlike single-turn jailbreaks that rely on one meticulously engineered prompt, Siege expands the conversation at each turn in a breadth-first fashion, branching out multiple adversarial prompts that exploit partial compliance from previous responses. By tracking these incremental policy leaks and re-injecting them into subsequent queries, Siege reveals how minor concessions can accumulate into fully disallowed outputs. Evaluations on the JailbreakBench dataset show that Siege achieves a 100% success rate on GPT-3.5-turbo and 97% on GPT-4 in a single multi-turn run, using fewer queries than baselines such as Crescendo or GOAT. This tree search methodology offers an in-depth view of how model safeguards degrade over successive dialogue turns, underscoring the urgency of robust multi-turn testing procedures for language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Siege," a novel framework for multi-turn jailbreaking of Large Language Models (LLMs).  Unlike single-turn attacks that rely on carefully crafted prompts, Siege adopts a tree search strategy.  At each turn in the conversation, it generates multiple adversarial prompts, branching out based on the model's responses. The framework tracks "partial compliance" – instances where the model provides some information that violates its safety guidelines, even if not a complete jailbreak.  These partial leaks are then "re-injected" into subsequent prompts to gradually erode the model's safety. Siege uses a breadth-first search (BFS) approach to explore different attack paths, pruning unpromising branches to maintain efficiency. The results on JailbreakBench demonstrates high success rates on models like GPT-3.5-turbo and GPT-4, often with fewer queries than existing multi-turn jailbreaking methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper combines several existing ideas in a new and potentially useful way. The use of tree search for adversarial attacks on LLMs is not entirely new, nor is the concept of multi-turn attacks. However, the integration of *explicit* partial compliance tracking and re-injection into a BFS framework represents a worthwhile innovation. Existing methods like Crescendo and GOAT explore multi-turn attacks but might not systematically explore multiple parallel attack vectors and track partial compliance as explicitly. The method of "re-injecting" partial compliance information into subsequent prompts feels more novel than the other parts of the approach.

* **Significance:**  The significance lies in its ability to reveal how vulnerabilities in LLM safety can accumulate over time, even from seemingly minor concessions. This highlights the need for more robust multi-turn testing procedures, as single-turn evaluations may not capture the full extent of a model's vulnerability. Demonstrating high success rates with fewer queries than other multi-turn methods is also significant as it makes evaluating LLMs more practical. The ability to quantify the degree of partial compliance is valuable for understanding the nuances of model behavior under adversarial pressure.  A practical aspect is the emphasis on closed-box evaluation, which is important because it evaluates LLMs in the mode of access that most users will encounter them.

* **Strengths:**
    * **High Success Rates:**  The paper achieves impressive jailbreaking success rates on challenging models like GPT-4.
    * **Efficiency:**  Siege uses fewer queries than other multi-turn methods, making it more practical for evaluation.
    * **Systematic Exploration:** The BFS-style tree search allows for a more thorough exploration of attack vectors than single-path approaches.
    * **Partial Compliance Tracking:** Explicitly quantifying and leveraging partial compliance is a key strength, offering a valuable metric for understanding model vulnerabilities.
    * **Closed-box Evaluation:** Evaluating models in a realistic closed-box environment is important for practical relevance.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of techniques is novel, the individual components have been explored in prior work.
    * **Generalizability of Findings:** The JailbreakBench dataset is a common benchmark, but the specific attack tactics and vulnerabilities discovered might not generalize perfectly to other models or adversarial scenarios.
    * **Limited Analysis:** The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of *why* specific attacks succeed or fail.  What types of partial compliance are most useful to exploit?  How do different branching strategies affect the overall success?
    * **Limited Real-world Validation:** The experiments are conducted within a simulated environment. Validating the framework in more realistic, user-driven scenarios would further strengthen the paper.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper's approach to multi-turn jailbreaking and partial compliance tracking could influence the development of more robust testing methodologies for LLMs. It could also inspire new defense mechanisms that are more resilient to iterative adversarial attacks.  The emphasis on efficiency is important for widespread adoption of such testing frameworks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a technically sound approach to a critical problem. The novel combination of tree search, partial compliance tracking, and reinjection offers a tangible advancement over existing methods. The empirical results are compelling, and the emphasis on efficiency is valuable. However, the incremental nature of the novelty, the limited in-depth analysis, and the lack of real-world validation prevent it from reaching a higher score. I believe that with more analysis on the dynamics of the tree search and attack vector success, as well as some real-world application, the impact of this approach would significantly increase.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### From TOWER to SPIRE: Adding the Speech Modality to a Text-Only LLM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10620v1)
- **Authors**: Kshitij Ambilduke, Ben Peters, Sonal Sannigrahi, Anil Keshwani, Tsz Kin Lam, Bruno Martins, Marcely Zanon Boito, André F. T. Martins
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown remarkable performance and generalization capabilities across multiple languages and tasks, making them very attractive targets for multi-modality integration (e.g., images or speech). In this work, we extend an existing LLM to the speech modality via speech discretization and continued pre-training. In particular, we are interested in multilingual LLMs, such as TOWER, as their pre-training setting allows us to treat discretized speech input as an additional translation language. The resulting open-source model, SPIRE, is able to transcribe and translate English speech input while maintaining TOWER's original performance on translation-related tasks, showcasing that discretized speech input integration as an additional language is feasible during LLM adaptation. We make our code and models available to the community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "From TOWER to SPIRE: Adding the Speech Modality to a Text-Only LLM":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SPIRE, a speech-augmented large language model (LLM) built upon the multilingual TOWER model. SPIRE is designed to incorporate speech processing capabilities while preserving TOWER's original performance on text-based tasks, particularly machine translation. The authors achieve this by discretizing speech into discrete speech units (DSUs) and treating these units as an additional "language" during continued pre-training (CPT). The model is then fine-tuned with both text and speech data via instruction tuning (IT). The resulting SPIRE model can perform English ASR and ST while maintaining TOWER's translation capabilities.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of this approach through experiments on ASR, MT, and ST tasks, showing competitive or superior performance compared to several baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The approach of adding speech to a multilingual LLM using discretized speech units is not entirely novel, as similar approaches exist (e.g., Spirit-LM). However, the paper's focus on maintaining the original model's text-based performance while adding speech capabilities is a distinguishing factor. The paper's two-stage training approach (CPT + IT) and the method of initializing the DSU embeddings are somewhat incremental improvements on existing techniques. The careful ablation studies investigating the impact of CPT and IT are valuable.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its demonstration that speech can be integrated into a pre-existing LLM *without* sacrificing its original text-based performance. This is a crucial point because many existing MLLMs prioritize speech-centric tasks at the expense of text performance. SPIRE's competitive performance on ASR and ST, along with its preservation of MT capabilities, makes it a potentially useful model. The reproducible pipeline and open-source release further enhance its impact.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-structured writing.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across ASR, MT, and ST tasks.
    *   Detailed ablation studies to understand the contribution of different training stages.
    *   Comparison to strong baselines, including Whisper and SeamlessM4T.
    *   Focus on multilingual capabilities.
    *   Open-source release of code and models.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The novelty is somewhat limited, as the general approach of using DSUs and CPT+IT has been explored before.
    *   While the ASR results are good, they don't significantly surpass the state-of-the-art, especially given that Whisper and SeamlessM4T are trained on significantly larger datasets.
    *   The gains in MT performance are modest.
    *   Some details, such as the hyperparameters used for the HuBERT and CTC models, are relegated to the appendix, which could be distracting.

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by showing that speech integration can be achieved without compromising text capabilities in LLMs. The focus on multilingual models, the careful experimental setup, and the open-source release are all commendable aspects. The primary limitations relate to incremental novelty compared to previous work, and the lack of large performance gains relative to existing SoTA models (though SPIRE requires significantly less speech data for training). While the component approaches are not entirely groundbreaking, the systematic analysis of the impact of each component, together with the emphasis on maintaining text quality, justifies a positive assessment.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Transformers without Normalization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10622v1)
- **Authors**: Jiachen Zhu, Xinlei Chen, Kaiming He, Yann LeCun, Zhuang Liu
- **Abstract**: Normalization layers are ubiquitous in modern neural networks and have long been considered essential. This work demonstrates that Transformers without normalization can achieve the same or better performance using a remarkably simple technique. We introduce Dynamic Tanh (DyT), an element-wise operation $DyT($x$) = \tanh(\alpha $x$)$, as a drop-in replacement for normalization layers in Transformers. DyT is inspired by the observation that layer normalization in Transformers often produces tanh-like, $S$-shaped input-output mappings. By incorporating DyT, Transformers without normalization can match or exceed the performance of their normalized counterparts, mostly without hyperparameter tuning. We validate the effectiveness of Transformers with DyT across diverse settings, ranging from recognition to generation, supervised to self-supervised learning, and computer vision to language models. These findings challenge the conventional understanding that normalization layers are indispensable in modern neural networks, and offer new insights into their role in deep networks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Dynamic Tanh (DyT), a simple, element-wise operation, `DyT(x) = tanh(ax)`, as a replacement for normalization layers in Transformers.  The authors observe that Layer Normalization (LN) in Transformers often produces tanh-like, S-shaped input-output mappings. By learning the scaling factor 'a', DyT emulates LN's behavior of scaling inputs and squashing extreme values without computing activation statistics. Experiments across various tasks (recognition, generation, supervised/self-supervised learning, vision/language models) demonstrate that Transformers with DyT can match or exceed the performance of their normalized counterparts, often without hyperparameter tuning.  The authors challenge the conventional understanding of the necessity of normalization layers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of replacing normalization layers with a scaled tanh activation is moderately novel. While the observation that LN produces tanh-like mappings is interesting, the leap to a direct substitution is relatively straightforward. The learnable scaling factor provides some adaptability, but the overall approach isn't fundamentally groundbreaking. Other methods have explored removing or modifying normalization layers, so this paper joins an existing line of inquiry.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact is significant if DyT holds up in a broader range of applications and scales well to even larger models. Demonstrating that Transformers can achieve comparable or better performance *without* normalization would challenge a long-held belief in the field. This could lead to simplified architectures, potential efficiency gains (as suggested by the latency measurements), and a better understanding of the true role of normalization. However, the reported gains are often marginal, and the experiments are not exhaustive across all possible tasks/datasets.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Simplicity:** DyT is remarkably simple and easy to implement.
    *   **Broad empirical validation:**  The paper presents a good range of experiments across different tasks, model types, and data domains.
    *   **Ablation studies:** The ablation studies provide some insights into the importance of both the tanh function and the learnable scaling parameter.
    *   **Efficiency claim:** The reported improvements in training and inference speed are a potentially significant benefit.
    *   **Well-written and clear:** The paper is easy to follow and understand.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental improvement:**  The performance gains from DyT over LN are often relatively small, and in some cases, DyT performs worse.  This raises the question of whether the complexity of adding DyT is justified.
    *   **Lack of theoretical justification:**  While the authors observe the similarity between LN and tanh, there's limited theoretical analysis explaining *why* DyT works.  A deeper understanding would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Limited hyperparameter tuning:** While the authors emphasize not tuning hyperparameters, this is a double-edged sword. It showcases DyT's robustness but also leaves open the possibility that LN *with* optimized hyperparameters could outperform DyT.
    *   **Dependence on existing architectures:** The study essentially *modifies* existing normalized architectures; it doesn't design novel architectures *around* DyT that might better leverage its properties.
    *   **Limited study of initialization:** The detailed study of a initialization only extends to the LLM setting. Understanding how the initial value impacts other tasks could be beneficial.
    *   **Negative result with BN replacement:** The failure of DyT to directly replace BN in classic ConvNets is a weakness. It suggests DyT isn't a universal replacement for all normalization techniques and highlights its potentially task-specific suitability.
    *   **LLM Scaling scalar:** A scalar learned directly after the embedding layers is added. This is a hyperparameter that should be further studied.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to encourage researchers to rethink the role of normalization layers and explore alternative architectural designs. If DyT or similar techniques prove robust and scalable, they could lead to more efficient and interpretable models.

*   **Score Justification:** The paper presents a moderately novel idea with promising empirical results across a reasonable range of tasks. While the performance gains are not always substantial and there are some limitations in the analysis, the potential impact of demonstrating normalization is not always necessary for training warrants a rating above average. The weaknesses are that the performance gains are marginal, there is a lack of theory to prove the claims, and there are negative results for Batch Normalization models. These elements reduce the score.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### NIL: No-data Imitation Learning by Leveraging Pre-trained Video Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10626v1)
- **Authors**: Mert Albaba, Chenhao Li, Markos Diomataris, Omid Taheri, Andreas Krause, Michael Black
- **Abstract**: Acquiring physically plausible motor skills across diverse and unconventional morphologies-including humanoid robots, quadrupeds, and animals-is essential for advancing character simulation and robotics. Traditional methods, such as reinforcement learning (RL) are task- and body-specific, require extensive reward function engineering, and do not generalize well. Imitation learning offers an alternative but relies heavily on high-quality expert demonstrations, which are difficult to obtain for non-human morphologies. Video diffusion models, on the other hand, are capable of generating realistic videos of various morphologies, from humans to ants. Leveraging this capability, we propose a data-independent approach for skill acquisition that learns 3D motor skills from 2D-generated videos, with generalization capability to unconventional and non-human forms. Specifically, we guide the imitation learning process by leveraging vision transformers for video-based comparisons by calculating pair-wise distance between video embeddings. Along with video-encoding distance, we also use a computed similarity between segmented video frames as a guidance reward. We validate our method on locomotion tasks involving unique body configurations. In humanoid robot locomotion tasks, we demonstrate that 'No-data Imitation Learning' (NIL) outperforms baselines trained on 3D motion-capture data. Our results highlight the potential of leveraging generative video models for physically plausible skill learning with diverse morphologies, effectively replacing data collection with data generation for imitation learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "NIL: No-data Imitation Learning by Leveraging Pre-trained Video Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach called No-data Imitation Learning (NIL) for training robots to perform tasks. NIL leverages pre-trained video diffusion models to generate reference videos of desired behaviors. These generated videos, combined with video vision transformers and image segmentation, provide a reward signal for imitation learning within a physics simulator. This allows robots to learn skills without relying on real-world or simulated expert demonstrations. The method is validated on locomotion tasks across various robot morphologies, demonstrating the ability to learn physically plausible skills from generated 2D videos and achieve performance comparable to motion capture data-based imitation learning baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of using video diffusion models to create training data for imitation learning is novel. Previous works in imitation learning heavily relied on curated expert data. Replacing data collection with data generation, particularly leveraging the recent advancements in video diffusion, is a significant shift. Furthermore, combining this with video vision transformers to create an informative reward signal represents a creative solution to a key challenge of learning from generated data.  The framework's ability to generalize to diverse robot morphologies is a major strength.  However, leveraging vision transformers to get a reward signal can be seen as more of an engineering contribution than a radically new conceptual breakthough. Also, video diffusion models are a relatively nascent field.
* **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to democratize robotics and character animation. By eliminating the need for costly and time-consuming data collection, NIL makes skill acquisition more accessible. The ability to generalize across diverse morphologies opens up possibilities for controlling robots with unconventional designs.  The experiments show that NIL can achieve performance comparable to imitation learning from motion capture data, which is a substantial achievement. However, the reliance on the _visual_ plausibility of the generated videos is a potential limitation. If the video diffusion models have biases towards unnatural movements the learned policy will inherit these biases. A deeper investigation into the physical plausibility of the generated videos could strengthen the claim. Also, the paper focuses on locomotion.  Extending the work to more complex manipulation tasks would be an important next step to prove generality.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Data independence:** Eliminates reliance on curated expert data.
    * **Generalization:** Applicable to diverse robot morphologies.
    * **Performance:** Achieves results comparable to motion capture-based imitation learning in locomotion tasks.
    * **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper addresses a clear and important problem in robotics.
    * **Strong Experimental Validation:** The paper includes comprehensive experiments to evaluate the approach. The comparisons to strong baselines, the ablation studies, and the evaluation across different robots all bolster the credibility of the work.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Visual Plausibility:** The approach depends on the visual quality of generated videos, which may not always translate to physical plausibility.
    * **Limited Tasks:**  The current evaluation focuses primarily on locomotion. The applicability to more complex manipulation tasks needs to be investigated.
    * **Potential for Bias:** The pre-trained video diffusion models might introduce biases in the learned policies.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a compelling solution to a significant challenge in robotics: the need for high-quality training data. NIL's innovative use of video diffusion models and video vision transformers represents a novel approach to generating training data and creating reward signals, respectively. This has the potential to lower the barrier to entry for robotic skill acquisition and enable control of robots with unconventional morphologies. The experimental results are promising, but the dependence on visual plausibility and the limited scope of tasks necessitate further research. Despite these weaknesses, the potential impact is considerable.
It's more an engineering contribution, taking components and putting them together in a valuable fashion, rather than groundbreaking.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### SciVerse: Unveiling the Knowledge Comprehension and Visual Reasoning of LMMs on Multi-modal Scientific Problems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10627v1)
- **Authors**: Ziyu Guo, Ray Zhang, Hao Chen, Jialin Gao, Dongzhi Jiang, Jiaze Wang, Pheng-Ann Heng
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of Large Multi-modal Models (LMMs) has enabled their application in scientific problem-solving, yet their fine-grained capabilities remain under-explored. In this paper, we introduce SciVerse, a multi-modal scientific evaluation benchmark to thoroughly assess LMMs across 5,735 test instances in five distinct versions. We aim to investigate three key dimensions of LMMs: scientific knowledge comprehension, multi-modal content interpretation, and Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. To unveil whether LMMs possess sufficient scientific expertise, we first transform each problem into three versions containing different levels of knowledge required for solving, i.e., Knowledge-free, -lite, and -rich. Then, to explore how LMMs interpret multi-modal scientific content, we annotate another two versions, i.e., Vision-rich and -only, marking more question information from texts to diagrams. Comparing the results of different versions, SciVerse systematically examines the professional knowledge stock and visual perception skills of LMMs in scientific domains. In addition, to rigorously assess CoT reasoning, we propose a new scientific CoT evaluation strategy, conducting a step-wise assessment on knowledge and logical errors in model outputs. Our extensive evaluation of different LMMs on SciVerse reveals critical limitations in their scientific proficiency and provides new insights into future developments. Project page: https://sciverse-cuhk.github.io
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SCIVERSE, a new multi-modal benchmark designed to evaluate the knowledge comprehension and visual reasoning capabilities of Large Multi-modal Models (LMMs) on scientific problems. The dataset consists of 5,735 test instances derived from 1,147 problems across physics, chemistry, and biology. SCIVERSE aims to assess three key dimensions: scientific knowledge comprehension (through variations in knowledge provided), multi-modal content interpretation (by varying information in text vs. diagrams), and Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning (using a novel step-wise evaluation strategy). The authors evaluate several open-source and closed-source LMMs on SCIVERSE and analyze their performance, revealing limitations in scientific proficiency and providing insights for future development.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses an important and relatively underexplored area: the application and evaluation of LMMs in scientific domains.  While LMMs are showing promise in various fields, their specific scientific reasoning capabilities need more rigorous scrutiny. SCIVERSE is designed to fill this gap, and its key strengths and weaknesses are:

**Strengths:**

*   **Targeted Evaluation:**  SCIVERSE directly tackles the issues of scientific knowledge understanding, multi-modal perception, and reasoning.  The structured creation of problem versions (Knowledge-free, -lite, -rich, Vision-rich, -only) is a valuable contribution, as it allows for the disentanglement of different aspects of LMM performance.
*   **Novel Evaluation Strategy for CoT:** The step-wise CoT evaluation, using GPT-4 to categorize steps and assess correctness, adds a significant layer of analysis. This approach provides richer insights compared to a simple binary accuracy metric.
*   **Dataset Size and Diversity:** The size of the dataset (5735 instances) and the inclusion of problems from physics, chemistry, and biology ensure a reasonably broad coverage of scientific domains.
*   **Identifies Critical Issues:** The paper effectively highlights that LMMs struggle significantly with Vision-only problems and showcases the superior CoT capabilities of closed-source models.
*   **Clearly Defined Research Questions:** The paper clearly articulates the key research questions that the benchmark is designed to address, providing a strong framework for the experiments and analysis.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Creation:** The paper doesn't provide many details about the dataset creation. More information would be beneficial concerning the quality and diversity of the original problems, selection criteria applied by science experts.
*   **Automatic CoT Evaluation Reliance:** Relying on GPT-4 for CoT evaluation may introduce bias or inconsistencies. While using a powerful model is convenient, a more robust validation of GPT-4's judgments would strengthen the conclusions.  Inter-rater reliability for GPT-4's classifications and evaluations should be mentioned.
*   **Limited Novelty in Problem Types:**  While the manipulation of knowledge and modality levels is novel, the core problem types might not be significantly different from existing scientific datasets.  The novelty primarily lies in the annotation and evaluation methodology.
*   **Complexity of Scientific Reasoning:**  The tasks, despite being college-level, might not fully capture the depth and complexity of scientific reasoning required in advanced research settings.
*   **Lack of ablation studies:** It is useful to include an ablation study about the different combinations of skills in the SCIVERSE dataset.

**Significance and Novelty:**

The significance lies in providing a structured benchmark for dissecting the scientific reasoning abilities of LMMs. The disentanglement of knowledge, modality, and reasoning errors is valuable. The SCIVERSE framework allows for a more nuanced understanding of LMM strengths and weaknesses in this domain. The novel stepwise CoT evaluation strategy strengthens the scientific rigor.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a useful and well-structured benchmark that addresses a relevant gap in the evaluation of LMMs.  The novel aspects of the benchmark, particularly the problem versioning and step-wise CoT evaluation, are the key contributions. However, the reliance on GPT-4 for CoT evaluation, and more comprehensive analysis about data quality needs to be addressed. While the core problem types might not be revolutionary, the systematic approach to evaluating LMMs across the defined dimensions is valuable for the community. SCIVERSE provides a solid foundation for future research on improving the scientific reasoning capabilities of LMMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Uncertainty in Action: Confidence Elicitation in Embodied Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10628v1)
- **Authors**: Tianjiao Yu, Vedant Shah, Muntasir Wahed, Kiet A. Nguyen, Adheesh Juvekar, Tal August, Ismini Lourentzou
- **Abstract**: Expressing confidence is challenging for embodied agents navigating dynamic multimodal environments, where uncertainty arises from both perception and decision-making processes. We present the first work investigating embodied confidence elicitation in open-ended multimodal environments. We introduce Elicitation Policies, which structure confidence assessment across inductive, deductive, and abductive reasoning, along with Execution Policies, which enhance confidence calibration through scenario reinterpretation, action sampling, and hypothetical reasoning. Evaluating agents in calibration and failure prediction tasks within the Minecraft environment, we show that structured reasoning approaches, such as Chain-of-Thoughts, improve confidence calibration. However, our findings also reveal persistent challenges in distinguishing uncertainty, particularly under abductive settings, underscoring the need for more sophisticated embodied confidence elicitation methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework, "Elicitation Policies," for improving confidence elicitation in embodied AI agents operating in complex, open-ended environments like Minecraft.  The framework structures confidence assessment using inductive, deductive, and abductive reasoning, enhanced by "Execution Policies" that refine calibration through scenario reinterpretation, action sampling, and hypothetical reasoning. Experiments in Minecraft show that structured reasoning (e.g., Chain-of-Thoughts) improves confidence calibration and failure prediction, but challenges remain in distinguishing uncertainty, especially under abductive conditions.  The paper analyzes embodied uncertainty, identifies effective methods for calibration and failure prediction, and highlights persistent challenges in this area.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents the *first* systematic investigation of embodied confidence elicitation in open-ended, multimodal environments like Minecraft. While confidence elicitation is explored in other machine learning contexts, extending it to embodied AI and introducing "Elicitation Policies" and "Execution Policies" demonstrates clear novelty. It addresses challenges specific to embodied AI, such as multimodal understanding, granularity of confidence, and interactive dependencies, setting it apart from prior works focused on traditional ML tasks or simpler environments. The structured analysis of uncertainty types (inductive, deductive, abductive) and their corresponding elicitation methods is also a valuable contribution.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial gap in embodied AI. As AI systems become more prevalent in real-world environments (e.g., autonomous driving), the ability to express and calibrate confidence is paramount for safety, reliability, and effective decision-making.  This work lays the groundwork for building more trustworthy and robust embodied AI agents.  The Minecraft environment, with its open-ended nature and complex interactions, is a compelling testbed. The experimental results provide insights into the strengths and limitations of different elicitation policies, which will be beneficial for future research. It points towards the need for adapting elicitation strategies based on the reasoning process of the embodied agent and its surroundings, which is a critical insight.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly defines the problem of confidence elicitation in embodied AI and identifies the unique challenges posed by open-ended multimodal environments.
    * **Framework Design:** The "Elicitation Policies" and "Execution Policies" provide a structured and intuitive way to approach confidence assessment and calibration.
    * **Experimental Evaluation:**  The Minecraft experiments are well-designed, using standard metrics (ECE, AUROC) and comparing different elicitation policies and embodied AI models (GPT-4V, MineLLM, STEVE). The ablation studies provide additional valuable insights into the contribution of various framework components.
    * **Insights and Analysis:**  The paper provides a nuanced analysis of the experimental results, highlighting the effectiveness of structured reasoning approaches (CoT, P&S), the challenges of abductive reasoning, and the differences in performance across various models.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Environmental Scope:** While Minecraft is a complex environment, it's still a simulation. Extending the framework to real-world robotics and physical environments would further validate its applicability.
    * **Model Dependency:**  The effectiveness of elicitation policies varies across different models (GPT-4V, MineLLM, STEVE). This highlights the need for developing more model-agnostic methods.
    * **Abductive Reasoning:** The persistent challenges in handling abductive uncertainty indicate a need for more sophisticated methods that go beyond simply generating multiple hypotheses (Top-K). Further research is needed to develop better elicitation policies for this reasoning type.
    * **Lack of Real-World Application:** The impact section hints toward real-world applications, which are only theoretical. The true impact of the proposed methodology is best evaluated through the design of real-world experiments.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of embodied AI by:
    * **Inspiring further research:**  It provides a starting point for exploring embodied confidence elicitation and motivates the development of new methods.
    * **Guiding the design of embodied AI systems:**  The framework and experimental results provide practical guidance for building more trustworthy and robust agents.
    * **Improving safety and reliability:**  By enabling agents to express and calibrate confidence, it can lead to safer and more reliable AI systems in real-world applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper addresses a novel and important problem in embodied AI. The proposed framework is well-designed, and the experimental results provide valuable insights. While the paper has some limitations (e.g., limited environmental scope, model dependency), its strengths outweigh its weaknesses. It provides a strong foundation for future research and has the potential to significantly impact the development of more trustworthy and robust embodied AI systems.  Therefore, the following score seems appropriate:

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### UniGoal: Towards Universal Zero-shot Goal-oriented Navigation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10630v1)
- **Authors**: Hang Yin, Xiuwei Xu, Lingqing Zhao, Ziwei Wang, Jie Zhou, Jiwen Lu
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we propose a general framework for universal zero-shot goal-oriented navigation. Existing zero-shot methods build inference framework upon large language models (LLM) for specific tasks, which differs a lot in overall pipeline and fails to generalize across different types of goal. Towards the aim of universal zero-shot navigation, we propose a uniform graph representation to unify different goals, including object category, instance image and text description. We also convert the observation of agent into an online maintained scene graph. With this consistent scene and goal representation, we preserve most structural information compared with pure text and are able to leverage LLM for explicit graph-based reasoning. Specifically, we conduct graph matching between the scene graph and goal graph at each time instant and propose different strategies to generate long-term goal of exploration according to different matching states. The agent first iteratively searches subgraph of goal when zero-matched. With partial matching, the agent then utilizes coordinate projection and anchor pair alignment to infer the goal location. Finally scene graph correction and goal verification are applied for perfect matching. We also present a blacklist mechanism to enable robust switch between stages. Extensive experiments on several benchmarks show that our UniGoal achieves state-of-the-art zero-shot performance on three studied navigation tasks with a single model, even outperforming task-specific zero-shot methods and supervised universal methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "UniGoal: Towards Universal Zero-shot Goal-oriented Navigation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UniGoal, a unified framework for zero-shot goal-oriented navigation that aims to work across different types of goals (object category, instance image, and text description). UniGoal uses a consistent graph representation for both the environment (scene graph) and the goal. It leverages a Large Language Model (LLM) for graph-based reasoning.  The core idea is to perform graph matching between the scene and goal graphs at each step and use different exploration strategies depending on the matching state. The approach includes iterative subgraph searching, coordinate projection, anchor pair alignment, scene graph correction, and goal verification. A blacklist mechanism is also used to prevent getting stuck in areas where matching has previously failed.  Experiments on multiple benchmarks (Matterport3D, HM3D, RoboTHOR) demonstrate state-of-the-art zero-shot performance compared to task-specific and supervised methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several aspects:
    *   **Unified Graph Representation:**  The use of a single graph representation to handle different goal types is a significant contribution.  Prior works often rely on task-specific approaches or text-based representations, which can limit generalization.
    *   **Multi-stage Exploration Policy:** The adaptive exploration strategy, switching between stages based on graph matching scores, is a clever way to guide the agent's behavior.  The stages are well-defined and conceptually clear.
    *   **Blacklist Mechanism:** This addition addresses the practical problem of agents getting stuck in unsuccessful areas.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The evaluation across multiple goal types and benchmarks strengthens the claim of universality.

*   **Significance:**  The work has the potential to significantly impact the field of robot navigation. A universal zero-shot method is highly desirable as it reduces the need for task-specific training and allows for more flexible interaction with humans. The use of LLMs for reasoning on scene and goal graphs is a promising direction. The performance improvements over existing zero-shot and even some supervised methods demonstrates the potential of the proposed approach.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing zero-shot methods and the need for a universal approach.
    *   **Well-Defined Approach:** UniGoal is well-explained with sufficient details about each component. The algorithm description and illustrations are helpful.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments provide compelling evidence that UniGoal achieves state-of-the-art performance on multiple tasks. The ablation studies effectively demonstrate the importance of each component.
    *   **Handles Multiple Goal Types:** A significant strength is its ability to handle object-goal, instance-image-goal and text-goal navigation seamlessly.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** Like many recent works in robotics, UniGoal relies on the reasoning abilities of LLMs. While LLMs are powerful, they are also known to be sensitive to prompting and can be unpredictable. This reliance could make the system less robust in real-world scenarios. The prompts are now provided in the supplementary material, which is good, but the sensitivity analysis isn't covered.
    *   **Complexity:** The pipeline is relatively complex, involving multiple stages, graph matching, LLM prompting, and various heuristics. This complexity could make it harder to debug and deploy in practice.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Failure Cases:** While the paper mentions failure causes, a deeper analysis of the common failure modes and potential solutions would be valuable. A discussion on the computational cost and scaling challenges would also be beneficial.
    *   **Dependency on scene graph:** As noted in the rebuttle, the system utilizes an online scene graph. What are the limitations with this dependency in real-world scenarios where perception will not be perfect?

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to influence future research in zero-shot navigation. The graph-based representation and multi-stage exploration policy provide a solid foundation for building more robust and generalizable navigation systems. Other researchers may adapt or extend UniGoal to handle more complex goals, dynamic environments, or incorporate other sensor modalities.

*   **Detailed Concerns Addressed in the Rebuttal:**
    *   The authors clarified the definition of zero-shot as "training-free" a distinction from ZSON and now revise the paper accordingly. This clarifies potential confusion around the label.
    *   The explanation of the efficiency improvement from the blacklist, blacklist increasing the SPL from 17.3 to 23.7, addresses concerns about efficiency.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of zero-shot robot navigation.  The unified approach to goal representation and the multi-stage exploration policy address a key limitation of existing methods.  The strong experimental results and comprehensive ablation studies provide compelling evidence of the effectiveness of UniGoal. While the reliance on LLMs and the complexity of the pipeline are potential weaknesses, the overall strengths of the paper outweigh these limitations. It has strong potential influence on the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### HybridVLA: Collaborative Diffusion and Autoregression in a Unified Vision-Language-Action Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10631v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaming Liu, Hao Chen, Pengju An, Zhuoyang Liu, Renrui Zhang, Chenyang Gu, Xiaoqi Li, Ziyu Guo, Sixiang Chen, Mengzhen Liu, Chengkai Hou, Mengdi Zhao, KC alex Zhou, Pheng-Ann Heng, Shanghang Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in vision-language models (VLMs) for common-sense reasoning have led to the development of vision-language-action (VLA) models, enabling robots to perform generalized manipulation. Although existing autoregressive VLA methods leverage large-scale pretrained knowledge, they disrupt the continuity of actions. Meanwhile, some VLA methods incorporate an additional diffusion head to predict continuous actions, relying solely on VLM-extracted features, which limits their reasoning capabilities. In this paper, we introduce HybridVLA, a unified framework that seamlessly integrates the strengths of both autoregressive and diffusion policies within a single large language model, rather than simply connecting them. To bridge the generation gap, a collaborative training recipe is proposed that injects the diffusion modeling directly into the next-token prediction. With this recipe, we find that these two forms of action prediction not only reinforce each other but also exhibit varying performance across different tasks. Therefore, we design a collaborative action ensemble mechanism that adaptively fuses these two predictions, leading to more robust control. In experiments, HybridVLA outperforms previous state-of-the-art VLA methods across various simulation and real-world tasks, including both single-arm and dual-arm robots, while demonstrating stable manipulation in previously unseen configurations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HybridVLA, a novel vision-language-action (VLA) model that unifies diffusion and autoregressive action prediction within a single large language model (LLM). Unlike previous approaches that treat diffusion as a separate module or quantize actions, HybridVLA integrates diffusion modeling directly into the LLM's next-token prediction process. A "collaborative training recipe" is proposed to inject diffusion-noised actions into the LLM's word embedding space. A collaborative action ensemble mechanism adaptively fuses the diffusion-based and autoregressive predictions based on confidence scores. The model is pre-trained on large-scale robotic datasets and fine-tuned on simulation and real-world data. Experimental results demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on various manipulation tasks with single-arm and dual-arm robots, showing good generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the unified architecture that tightly integrates diffusion and autoregressive action prediction within a single LLM.  Existing methods tend to either append diffusion heads or quantize actions. The collaborative training recipe and the adaptive fusion mechanism are also novel contributions. While diffusion models for robotics are not entirely new, this specific integration with autoregressive LLMs for VLAs is a distinct advancement. The idea of inject diffusion noise into the LLM embeddings is creative.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in VLA: balancing the continuous nature of actions (handled well by diffusion) with the reasoning capabilities of LLMs (leveraged by autoregressive models). The proposed architecture seems to effectively combine these strengths, leading to improved performance and generalization. The comprehensive experimental results across diverse tasks and real-world scenarios strengthen the significance of the findings.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Unified Architecture:**  The core idea of unifying diffusion and autoregressive prediction in a single LLM is well-motivated and appears effective.
    *   **Collaborative Training Recipe:** This is a key enabler for the unified architecture, bridging the gap between the two action generation approaches.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The experiments are thorough, covering simulation, real-world tasks, and generalization tests. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contributions of different components.
    *   **Strong Results:**  The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance compared to existing VLA methods.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Inference Speed:** The autoregressive component can be a bottleneck, potentially limiting real-time control frequency, although they address this by using a diffusion-only version and KV caching. This trade-off between accuracy and speed needs to be carefully considered.
    *   **Complexity:** Integrating two different generation methods inevitably increases the complexity of the architecture and training process. A simpler architecture with competitive performance would be preferable.
    *   **Limited Explanation:** While the authors mention the benefits of collaborative training and ensembling, it might have been useful to provide a deeper dive into *why* certain tasks benefit from either diffusion or autoregressive actions. This may further optimize future models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the VLA field by promoting a more integrated approach to combining diffusion and autoregressive models. The proposed architecture and training recipe could serve as a foundation for future research in this area. It also could be used to build more powerful and robust robots with a wide range of applications.

*   **Justification:** The paper provides a significant contribution to the field by effectively unifying diffusion and autoregressive action prediction within a single LLM framework for VLA. Although the model is complex, the results from the comprehensive experiments confirm the effectiveness of the proposed approach in enhancing performance and generalization.
