- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Learning to Inference Adaptively for Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10905v1)
- **Authors**: Zhuoyan Xu, Khoi Duc Nguyen, Preeti Mukherjee, Saurabh Bagchi, Somali Chaterji, Yingyu Liang, Yin Li
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have shown impressive capabilities in reasoning, yet come with substantial computational cost, limiting their deployment in resource-constrained settings. Despite recent efforts on improving the efficiency of MLLMs, prior solutions fall short in responding to varying runtime conditions, in particular changing resource availability (e.g., contention due to the execution of other programs on the device). To bridge this gap, we introduce AdaLLaVA, an adaptive inference framework that learns to dynamically reconfigure operations in an MLLM during inference, accounting for the input data and a latency budget. We conduct extensive experiments across benchmarks involving question-answering, reasoning, and hallucination. Our results show that AdaLLaVA effectively adheres to input latency budget, achieving varying accuracy and latency tradeoffs at runtime. Further, we demonstrate that AdaLLaVA adapts to both input latency and content, can be integrated with token selection for enhanced efficiency, and generalizes across MLLMs.Our project webpage with code release is at https://zhuoyan-xu.github.io/ada-llava/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AdaLLaVA, an adaptive inference framework for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). AdaLLaVA aims to dynamically reconfigure the operations within an MLLM during inference, taking into account both input data and a predefined latency budget. The core idea is to treat an MLLM as a collection of shallower models and learn a scheduler that selects a subset of operations (Transformer blocks or attention heads) based on the input content and the latency constraint. This allows for accuracy-latency trade-offs at runtime.  The scheduler is trained using a probabilistic modeling approach that incorporates latency constraints, and the method is shown to be compatible with token selection techniques. Extensive experiments demonstrate AdaLLaVA's ability to adapt to varying latency requirements while maintaining performance and generalize across different MLLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to adaptive inference for MLLMs. While adaptive inference techniques exist for other types of models, the specific application to MLLMs with a focus on latency-aware dynamic reconfiguration is relatively new. The learning-based scheduler and the probabilistic modeling approach for incorporating latency constraints during training are also key innovative components.  Previous work had explored bypassing transformer blocks to reduce latency, but AdaLLaVA combines this insight with input-content awareness and a learned scheduler, leading to a more general and effective solution.

*   **Significance:**  The work addresses a practical challenge in deploying MLLMs in resource-constrained settings or environments with fluctuating resource availability.  The ability to dynamically adapt the model's computational load based on both input content and latency constraints makes MLLMs more versatile and applicable in real-world scenarios. The reported performance improvements in terms of efficiency with minimal accuracy loss are significant and could have a practical impact. The ability to integrate with token selection methods further enhances its utility. The idea of dynamic execution plans tailored for input images seems to be beneficial.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The approach is well-motivated and addresses a real-world problem.
    *   The technical approach is sound, with a clear explanation of the scheduler and training process.
    *   Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of AdaLLaVA.
    *   The integration with token selection techniques further enhances the value of the work.
    *   The content awareness aspect with visualizing attention maps offers great insights.
    *   Clear exposition and well-structured presentation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper demonstrates good performance across several datasets, it could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the types of inputs or scenarios where AdaLLaVA provides the greatest advantage.  What types of images/queries are best suited for this technique? A failure case analysis could also improve the paper.
    *   The paper focuses on algorithmic-level innovation and acknowledges the need for system-level serving optimization.  Further work is needed to address the practical challenges of deploying AdaLLaVA in a real-world serving environment.

*   **Potential Influence:** AdaLLaVA has the potential to influence the development of more efficient and adaptable MLLM inference techniques. It could inspire further research into dynamic reconfiguration strategies and learning-based schedulers for optimizing MLLM performance. The paper's emphasis on latency-aware inference is likely to become increasingly important as MLLMs are deployed in more real-world applications.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

While the core idea is relatively simple, AdaLLaVA presents a meaningful advance in MLLM inference efficiency.  The strengths of the paper – particularly its practical motivation, sound methodology, strong empirical validation, integration with existing techniques, and the content-aware nature of the approach – substantially outweigh the weaknesses.  The paper provides valuable insights and a working framework for improving MLLM efficiency, opening avenues for follow-up works.

- **Score**: 8/10

### OASST-ETC Dataset: Alignment Signals from Eye-tracking Analysis of LLM Responses
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10927v1)
- **Authors**: Angela Lopez-Cardona, Sebastian Idesis, Miguel Barreda-Ángeles, Sergi Abadal, Ioannis Arapakis
- **Abstract**: While Large Language Models (LLMs) have significantly advanced natural language processing, aligning them with human preferences remains an open challenge. Although current alignment methods rely primarily on explicit feedback, eye-tracking (ET) data offers insights into real-time cognitive processing during reading. In this paper, we present OASST-ETC, a novel eye-tracking corpus capturing reading patterns from 24 participants, while evaluating LLM-generated responses from the OASST1 dataset. Our analysis reveals distinct reading patterns between preferred and non-preferred responses, which we compare with synthetic eye-tracking data. Furthermore, we examine the correlation between human reading measures and attention patterns from various transformer-based models, discovering stronger correlations in preferred responses. This work introduces a unique resource for studying human cognitive processing in LLM evaluation and suggests promising directions for incorporating eye-tracking data into alignment methods. The dataset and analysis code are publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the OASST-ETC dataset, a novel eye-tracking (ET) corpus focused on understanding alignment signals in Large Language Model (LLM) responses. The dataset captures reading patterns from 24 participants evaluating LLM-generated responses from the OASST1 dataset. The analysis reveals distinct reading patterns between preferred and non-preferred responses and compares them to synthetic ET data. Furthermore, the paper examines the correlation between human reading measures and attention patterns from transformer-based models, finding stronger correlations in preferred responses. The authors argue that their dataset and analysis provide a unique resource for studying human cognitive processing during LLM evaluation and suggest directions for incorporating ET data into alignment methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The OASST-ETC dataset is the first of its kind, providing valuable real-time behavioral data (eye movements) related to human evaluation of LLM outputs. This fills a gap in the existing research, which primarily relies on explicit feedback.
    *   **Methodology:** The paper uses a well-defined experimental setup and standard ET data processing techniques. The comparison of preferred and non-preferred responses is a good starting point for understanding alignment. The inclusion of synthetic ET data and analysis of model attention patterns adds depth to the study.
    *   **Relevance:** The topic is highly relevant to the ongoing efforts to align LLMs with human preferences and values. The potential for using implicit feedback from ET to improve alignment methods is promising.
    *   **Open Science:** The authors make the dataset and analysis code publicly available, enhancing reproducibility and facilitating further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scale:** The dataset has a relatively small number of participants (24) and prompts, potentially limiting the generalizability of the findings. While a practical starting point, it is essential to scale up for more robust results.
    *   **Dataset Bias:** The focus on English-language texts and participants with high English proficiency may introduce bias. Exploring other languages and populations would be valuable.
    *   **Generative model for reading measures**: The paper finds differences between ET signals from real participants and signals generated through RoBERTa-based model, suggesting limited reliability of the model for ET prediction
    *   **Correlations vs Causation:** The study primarily focuses on correlations between reading measures and model attention. While interesting, it doesn't establish causal relationships or demonstrate how ET data can directly improve LLM alignment.
    *   **Limited Exploration of LLM Alignment:** The paper shows interesting correlations, but it doesn't propose specific algorithms or methods for directly incorporating ET data into RLHF or other alignment techniques. The practical impact on LLM alignment remains to be demonstrated.
    *   **Model Task**: Contrary to expectations, task-specific alignment did not consistently improve correlation strength. The standard, pretrained Llama 3.1 model, which lacked alignment or instruction-following tuning, generally achieved higher correlations than its instruction-tuned counterparts.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper opens up new avenues for research on LLM alignment by introducing a novel data source and analysis approach.
    *   It contributes to our understanding of how humans process and evaluate LLM-generated text.
    *   The findings can inform the development of more sophisticated alignment methods that go beyond explicit feedback.
    *   The public availability of the dataset is a valuable resource for the research community.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence future research by:

    *   Encouraging the development of larger and more diverse ET datasets for LLM evaluation.
    *   Inspiring the creation of new alignment algorithms that incorporate implicit feedback from ET or other behavioral data.
    *   Motivating further investigation of the relationship between human cognitive processes and model attention mechanisms.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper presents a valuable and novel dataset, the small scale, potential biases, and lack of direct application to LLM alignment limit its immediate impact. The correlations found are interesting but need further investigation to establish causal relationships and practical utility. However, the paper's contribution to opening up a new research area and the public availability of the dataset justify a relatively good score.

Score: 6. The paper introduces a novel dataset with relevant implications. The limitations can be overcome in future work, which might lead to a higher impact score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### ChatGPT Encounters Morphing Attack Detection: Zero-Shot MAD with Multi-Modal Large Language Models and General Vision Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10937v1)
- **Authors**: Haoyu Zhang, Raghavendra Ramachandra, Kiran Raja, Christoph Busch
- **Abstract**: Face Recognition Systems (FRS) are increasingly vulnerable to face-morphing attacks, prompting the development of Morphing Attack Detection (MAD) algorithms. However, a key challenge in MAD lies in its limited generalizability to unseen data and its lack of explainability-critical for practical application environments such as enrolment stations and automated border control systems. Recognizing that most existing MAD algorithms rely on supervised learning paradigms, this work explores a novel approach to MAD using zero-shot learning leveraged on Large Language Models (LLMs). We propose two types of zero-shot MAD algorithms: one leveraging general vision models and the other utilizing multimodal LLMs. For general vision models, we address the MAD task by computing the mean support embedding of an independent support set without using morphed images. For the LLM-based approach, we employ the state-of-the-art GPT-4 Turbo API with carefully crafted prompts. To evaluate the feasibility of zero-shot MAD and the effectiveness of the proposed methods, we constructed a print-scan morph dataset featuring various unseen morphing algorithms, simulating challenging real-world application scenarios. Experimental results demonstrated notable detection accuracy, validating the applicability of zero-shot learning for MAD tasks. Additionally, our investigation into LLM-based MAD revealed that multimodal LLMs, such as ChatGPT, exhibit remarkable generalizability to untrained MAD tasks. Furthermore, they possess a unique ability to provide explanations and guidance, which can enhance transparency and usability for end-users in practical applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper explores a novel approach to Morphing Attack Detection (MAD) using zero-shot learning, leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs). It addresses the limitations of existing supervised MAD algorithms, such as their limited generalization to unseen data and lack of explainability. The authors propose two types of zero-shot MAD algorithms: one utilizing general vision models and another utilizing multimodal LLMs like GPT-4 Turbo. For the vision model approach, they compute the mean support embedding from a set of digital bona fide images. For the LLM approach, they carefully craft prompts to guide the LLM in classifying print-scanned morphed and non-morphed face images.  They construct a print-scan morph dataset with various unseen morphing algorithms to simulate real-world scenarios.  The experiments demonstrate the feasibility of zero-shot MAD and highlight the generalizability and explainability capabilities of LLMs, particularly ChatGPT.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the application of zero-shot learning, specifically leveraging LLMs, to the MAD task.  While LLMs have been explored in other biometric areas and digital forgery detection to a limited degree, this paper represents the first comprehensive exploration of LLMs for *S-MAD,* especially with a focus on print-scanned data which is important to real-world applications. The prompt engineering strategies for reliable morphing attack detection are also novel and contribute significantly. The use of a supporting set to provide context for the vision-model zero-shot approach is another innovative aspect. The comparative evaluation between LLM-based and vision-model-based zero-shot methods is valuable.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the potential to address the limitations of current supervised MAD algorithms:

    *   **Generalization:**  Zero-shot learning offers a way to improve generalization to unseen morphing attacks without requiring task-specific training data.  This is crucial because the morphing attack landscape is constantly evolving.
    *   **Explainability:**  LLMs can provide explanations for their classifications, enhancing transparency and trust in MAD systems. This explainability is especially critical in sensitive applications like border control.
    *   **Practicality:**  The focus on print-scanned data makes the research relevant to real-world scenarios where image quality is often limited.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem and motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges in MAD and justifies the need for a zero-shot approach.
    *   **Thorough experimental design:** The construction of a custom dataset featuring various morphing algorithms and print-scan simulation is a strong point. The evaluation metrics are standard and appropriate.
    *   **Comprehensive analysis:** The paper goes beyond simple accuracy metrics to examine the consistency, stability, and explainability of the LLM-based approach. The prompt engineering study is insightful.
    *   **Comparative evaluation:** The comparison with the vision-model-based zero-shot method provides a useful benchmark.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Synthetic Data:** The use of synthetic data, while necessary for privacy compliance and LLM evaluation, limits the conclusions that can be drawn about real-world performance. While the authors simulate print/scan degradations, it might not fully capture the complexities of authentic border control images.
    *   **Reliance on specific LLMs:** The paper primarily relies on GPT-4 Turbo. The generalizability of the findings to other LLMs isn't fully explored.
    *   **Limited scale of the analysis:** The qualitative analysis of explanations is somewhat limited. A more in-depth exploration of LLM failure cases and the nuances of the explanations would be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in MAD by:

    *   Inspiring more work on zero-shot learning for MAD.
    *   Promoting the use of LLMs for explainable and adaptable MAD systems.
    *   Highlighting the importance of evaluating MAD algorithms on print-scanned data.
    *   Encouraging further investigation of prompt engineering techniques for LLMs in biometric applications.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by being the first to comprehensively explore LLMs for zero-shot S-MAD, particularly with a focus on print-scanned data and explainability. The novelty is strong, and the potential impact on the field is considerable. While the reliance on synthetic data and the limited scale of some analyses are weaknesses, the overall strengths of the paper outweigh these limitations. The rigorous experimental design and detailed analysis provide a solid foundation for future research in this area. The paper has the potential to shift the paradigm in MAD towards more adaptable, explainable, and practical solutions.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Graph-Grounded LLMs: Leveraging Graphical Function Calling to Minimize LLM Hallucinations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10941v1)
- **Authors**: Piyush Gupta, Sangjae Bae, David Isele
- **Abstract**: The adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) is rapidly expanding across various tasks that involve inherent graphical structures. Graphs are integral to a wide range of applications, including motion planning for autonomous vehicles, social networks, scene understanding, and knowledge graphs. Many problems, even those not initially perceived as graph-based, can be effectively addressed through graph theory. However, when applied to these tasks, LLMs often encounter challenges, such as hallucinations and mathematical inaccuracies. To overcome these limitations, we propose Graph-Grounded LLMs, a system that improves LLM performance on graph-related tasks by integrating a graph library through function calls. By grounding LLMs in this manner, we demonstrate significant reductions in hallucinations and improved mathematical accuracy in solving graph-based problems, as evidenced by the performance on the NLGraph benchmark. Finally, we showcase a disaster rescue application where the Graph-Grounded LLM acts as a decision-support system.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can summarize the paper, provide a critical evaluation, and assign a novelty/significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper "Graph-Grounded LLMs: Leveraging Graphical Function Calling to Minimize LLM Hallucinations" introduces a system called Graph-Grounded LLMs. This system aims to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) on tasks involving graphs by integrating a graph library accessible through function calls.  The core idea is to leverage LLMs for high-level reasoning while offloading computationally intensive graph operations to traditional graph algorithms implemented in a library like `networkx`. The authors demonstrate that this approach significantly reduces hallucinations and improves accuracy on graph-related tasks using the NLGraph benchmark. They also showcase a disaster response application where the Graph-Grounded LLM acts as a decision-support system, gathering environmental data, planning responses, and answering related queries.  The key benefits highlighted are reduced LLM overhead, scalability to larger graphs, robust edge-case handling, and error-guided self-correction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies a critical weakness of LLMs – their struggle with graph-related tasks due to hallucinations and inaccuracies. This is a well-known limitation.
    *   **Well-Defined Solution:** The Graph-Grounded LLM approach offers a pragmatic and effective solution by combining the strengths of LLMs (high-level reasoning) with the reliability and efficiency of traditional graph algorithms. The function calling mechanism is a good way to delegate the task.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The NLGraph benchmark provides a rigorous evaluation framework, and the results clearly demonstrate the superiority of the Graph-Grounded LLM over standalone LLMs in almost all tasks. The detailed failure case analysis is valuable for identifying limitations and potential improvements.
    *   **Practical Application:** The disaster response application provides a compelling demonstration of the real-world potential of the proposed system. The scenario helps understand how graph theory provides structure for complicated problems and LLMs provide the high level decision-making.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty:** The idea of combining LLMs with external tools or libraries is not entirely new (function calling is an established technique).  The core novelty lies in the specific application to graph-related tasks and the detailed implementation and evaluation.
    *   **Reliance on Hand-Crafted Graph Library:** The system depends on a carefully designed and implemented graph library. This requires significant upfront engineering effort and may limit the adaptability to domains where a suitable library doesn't already exist. Furthermore, there is no automated way to create graph libraries. The user needs to create each function by hand.
    *   **Computational Overhead:** While the paper acknowledges the increased computation time due to multiple function calls, the impact on real-time applications could be a significant bottleneck. The computation time increase might negate the benefits of using LLMs in some scenarios.
    *   **Context Window Limitations:** The context window is a limitation with most LLMs, and is exacerbated with increased information from the graph library.
    *   **NP-Complete Problem Implementation:** The mention of an NP-complete problem implementation and its inefficiency in the evaluation section reveals a possible weakness in the graph library. The library needs to have more efficient implementations for these problems.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper provides a valuable contribution to the field by demonstrating a practical way to overcome the limitations of LLMs in graph-related tasks.
    *   The Graph-Grounded LLM approach has the potential to significantly improve the reliability and accuracy of LLM-based systems in various domains, including robotics, engineering, and disaster management.
    *   The detailed evaluation and failure case analysis offer valuable insights for future research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7** out of 10.

*   The approach is practical and well-evaluated. It demonstrably improves performance on a relevant problem.
*   While the core idea of function calling isn't novel, the specific application to graphs, the comprehensive evaluation using NLGraph, and the disaster response application showcase a valuable and well-executed contribution.
*   The reliance on a hand-crafted graph library and the computational overhead are significant limitations that detract from the novelty and overall impact. The lack of automatic graph library expansion is also a concern. The implementation of an NP-Complete problem within the graph library is also concerning.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Predicting Stock Movement with BERTweet and Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10957v1)
- **Authors**: Michael Charles Albada, Mojolaoluwa Joshua Sonola
- **Abstract**: Applying deep learning and computational intelligence to finance has been a popular area of applied research, both within academia and industry, and continues to attract active attention. The inherently high volatility and non-stationary of the data pose substantial challenges to machine learning models, especially so for today's expressive and highly-parameterized deep learning models. Recent work has combined natural language processing on data from social media to augment models based purely on historic price data to improve performance has received particular attention. Previous work has achieved state-of-the-art performance on this task by combining techniques such as bidirectional GRUs, variational autoencoders, word and document embeddings, self-attention, graph attention, and adversarial training. In this paper, we demonstrated the efficacy of BERTweet, a variant of BERT pre-trained specifically on a Twitter corpus, and the transformer architecture by achieving competitive performance with the existing literature and setting a new baseline for Matthews Correlation Coefficient on the Stocknet dataset without auxiliary data sources.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of BERTweet, a variant of BERT pre-trained on Twitter data, and transformer architectures for predicting stock market movements. The authors aim to improve performance on the Stocknet dataset by leveraging natural language processing of social media data (tweets) in conjunction with historical price data.  They experiment with several model architectures, including feed-forward networks, transformer encoders replacing variational movement decoders, BERTweet transformers, and cross-attention transformers. They achieve competitive performance with existing literature and establish a new baseline for Matthews Correlation Coefficient (MCC) on the Stocknet dataset *without* the use of auxiliary data sources. The experiments involve hyperparameter tuning and careful consideration of data loading and training stability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of this paper lies in its *application* of BERTweet and specific transformer architectures to the stock market prediction task using the Stocknet dataset. While BERTweet and transformers themselves are not novel, their direct application to Stocknet, particularly in a setting that avoids relying on auxiliary datasets, is a contribution. Prior work had used RNNs, LSTMs, GRUs and other techniques for this task. Using BERTweet in this domain is a worthwhile exploration since BERTweet is a specifically trained NLP model for tweets. However, the core ideas are not revolutionary.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is somewhat limited but not insignificant. Achieving competitive performance on the Stocknet dataset is a valuable step. Improving the MCC benchmark for the dataset using *only* the existing data shows a better use of existing information. Showing competitive accuracy as previous papers helps to show the use of other features is not necessary. However, it should be noted there are some papers which this paper does not match performance (See Table 2).

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and well-defined experimental setup using the Stocknet dataset.
    *   Exploration of multiple transformer architectures, including cross-attention.
    *   Careful consideration of data loading and training stability issues and discussion.
    *   Achieves a new MCC baseline without relying on auxiliary datasets.
    *   The writing is clear and the paper provides enough information to understand the work performed.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The gains in MCC over the previous baseline are marginal improvements.
    *   The novelty is primarily in the *application* of existing methods, rather than the development of fundamentally new techniques.
    *   Performance is not always as good or better than alternative methods shown in Table 2, but has the bonus of only using tweets and historical price data.
    *   The paper does not provide extensive analysis on *why* certain architectures and hyperparameters worked better than others. Further investigation into feature importances, attention maps, or error analysis would strengthen the analysis.
    *   The work is specifically applied to the Stocknet dataset, thus, it may not be fully generalizable.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in this area by encouraging the use of BERTweet and transformers as strong baseline models for stock market prediction. Also the importance of the dataset can be more thoroughly extracted if given good tools to do it. Its contribution is also in the field of general machine learning due to the exploration of some of the difficulties in doing time series and deep learning due to the sensitivity of training on a low signal to noise ratio data.

**Score:** 6

**Rationale:**

The paper offers a competent application of BERTweet and transformers to a challenging problem. It achieves a new benchmark but only without the use of other features. The significance is present but does not have high impact. The weaknesses are primarily in the incremental nature of the work, limited analysis of the results, and not fully exceeding performance of previous methods. The paper showcases the value of combining NLP and time series, but doesn't offer a groundbreaking shift in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Combinatorial Optimization for All: Using LLMs to Aid Non-Experts in Improving Optimization Algorithms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10968v1)
- **Authors**: Camilo Chacón Sartori, Christian Blum
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown notable potential in code generation for optimization algorithms, unlocking exciting new opportunities. This paper examines how LLMs, rather than creating algorithms from scratch, can improve existing ones without the need for specialized expertise. To explore this potential, we selected 10 baseline optimization algorithms from various domains (metaheuristics, reinforcement learning, deterministic, and exact methods) to solve the classic Travelling Salesman Problem. The results show that our simple methodology often results in LLM-generated algorithm variants that improve over the baseline algorithms in terms of solution quality, reduction in computational time, and simplification of code complexity, all without requiring specialized optimization knowledge or advanced algorithmic implementation skills.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how Large Language Models (LLMs) can be used to enhance existing optimization algorithms for the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) without requiring specialized expertise from the user. The authors take 10 baseline algorithms from various domains (metaheuristics, reinforcement learning, deterministic heuristics, and exact methods), sourced from the `pyCombinatorial` library, and use LLMs to suggest code improvements through a chatbot interface. The LLMs are prompted to incorporate state-of-the-art techniques focusing on improving solution quality and accelerating convergence. The paper then analyzes the LLM-generated algorithm variants, comparing them to the original algorithms in terms of solution quality, computational time, and code complexity.  Results show that the LLM-generated variants often improve upon the baseline algorithms.  The paper explores the reasons behind the improvements, highlighting instances where LLMs introduce hybrid initialization, adaptive cooling schedules, and Boltzmann exploration. The impact on code complexity, as measured by cyclomatic complexity, is also analyzed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its application of LLMs to improve existing optimization algorithms *without* requiring expert knowledge from the user. Previous works have focused on LLMs generating algorithms from scratch or analyzing existing algorithms.  Using LLMs as a tool to assist non-experts in enhancing existing implementations is a significant shift. The systematic evaluation of this approach across a diverse set of algorithms for a specific, well-known problem (TSP) provides a solid foundation.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact is significant. If successful, this approach could democratize access to high-performance optimization techniques, allowing individuals without deep optimization expertise to tackle complex problems more effectively.  The paper's findings suggest that LLMs can indeed identify and incorporate relevant algorithmic improvements.  However, it is important to note that the study is limited to the TSP, and the results may not generalize to all optimization problems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper's systematic methodology, testing a wide range of algorithms and LLMs, is commendable.
    *   The analysis of *why* certain LLM-generated codes outperform the original ones is insightful.  The identification of specific techniques like hybrid initialization and Boltzmann exploration adds value.
    *   The exploration of code complexity as a metric is a useful addition, addressing not just performance but also maintainability and readability.
    *   The reproducibility via a chatbot enhances the value and accessibility of the work.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The study is limited to the TSP. While a canonical problem, it is crucial to understand how well the findings generalize to other, more complex optimization problems.
    *   The reliance on `pyCombinatorial` for baseline algorithms introduces a potential bias. The quality and specific implementation choices within this library might influence the improvements suggested by the LLMs.
    *   The paper acknowledges the lack of precise source attribution for code updates made by LLMs, making it challenging to fully understand and validate the origins of the improvements.

*   **Score Justification:**
    *   The paper addresses a timely and relevant topic. LLMs hold immense potential for augmenting human capabilities in various domains, including optimization.
    *   While the study is confined to TSP and uses a specific library, it offers a solid proof-of-concept and establishes a methodology for using LLMs to enhance existing algorithms.
    *   The detailed analysis of improvements, and consideration of code complexity, adds depth to the evaluation.
    *   The approach has practical potential for democratizing access to advanced optimization techniques.

Considering the above points, I believe this paper has good novelty but leaves room for a deeper understanding of the generalizability of its findings.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Dionysius Emerges Apollo -- Learning Patterns and Abstractions from Perceptual Sequences
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10973v1)
- **Authors**: Shuchen Wu
- **Abstract**: Cognition swiftly breaks high-dimensional sensory streams into familiar parts and uncovers their relations. Why do structures emerge, and how do they enable learning, generalization, and prediction? What computational principles underlie this core aspect of perception and intelligence? A sensory stream, simplified, is a one-dimensional sequence. In learning such sequences, we naturally segment them into parts -- a process known as chunking. In the first project, I investigated factors influencing chunking in a serial reaction time task and showed that humans adapt to underlying chunks while balancing speed and accuracy. Building on this, I developed models that learn chunks and parse sequences chunk by chunk. Normatively, I proposed chunking as a rational strategy for discovering recurring patterns and nested hierarchies, enabling efficient sequence factorization. Learned chunks serve as reusable primitives for transfer, composition, and mental simulation -- letting the model compose the new from the known. I demonstrated this model's ability to learn hierarchies in single and multi-dimensional sequences and highlighted its utility for unsupervised pattern discovery. The second part moves from concrete to abstract sequences. I taxonomized abstract motifs and examined their role in sequence memory. Behavioral evidence suggests that humans exploit pattern redundancies for compression and transfer. I proposed a non-parametric hierarchical variable model that learns both chunks and abstract variables, uncovering invariant symbolic patterns. I showed its similarity to human learning and compared it to large language models. Taken together, this thesis suggests that chunking and abstraction as simple computational principles enable structured knowledge acquisition in hierarchically organized sequences, from simple to complex, concrete to abstract.
- **Summary**: This dissertation investigates how humans and computational models can learn patterns and abstractions from perceptual sequences. It explores chunking as a fundamental mechanism for segmenting sensory input into meaningful units and forming structured representations. The work is divided into three main parts: (1) exploring chunking in serial reaction time tasks, demonstrating how humans adapt chunking strategies and build a rational model to balance speed and accuracy; (2) examining chunking from a computational and normative perspective, proposing a generative model that unearths hierarchical patterns within sequences; and (3) moving beyond concrete sequences to capture more abstract motifs, with experiments and models suggesting a deeper, transferable chunking process and layering abstraction over concrete chunking. The overarching theme is to demonstrate how chunking scaffolds an agent's understanding of the world and forms the basis for a structured world model learned through experience.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The dissertation presents a cohesive and well-structured investigation into the role of chunking and abstraction in sequence learning, with a focus on integrating computational modeling and behavioral experiments. The strength of this work lies in its systematic approach, starting from basic chunking mechanisms in simple tasks and progressively building towards more complex and abstract representations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Systematic progression:** The work demonstrates a clear progression from concrete examples to abstract concepts, building upon previous findings and addressing limitations in a logical manner.
    *   **Integration of methods:** The integration of behavioral experiments and computational modeling provides a strong empirical foundation for the theoretical claims.
    *   **Normative perspective:** The emphasis on a normative, rational approach to chunking offers a compelling argument for why humans engage in this behavior, connecting it to efficient compression and generalization.
    *   **Novel models:** Development of the Hierarchical Chunking Model (HCM) and Hierarchical Variable Learning Model (HVM), shows effort in constructing complex representations.
    * The comparison of HCM and HVM with other large language models show that HCM and HVM is effective at capturing chunking behavior.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The focus on one-dimensional discrete sequences, while a simplified starting point, might limit the generalizability of the findings to more complex, real-world scenarios, visual or audio-based, etc.
    *   **Greedy parsing strategy:** The reliance on a greedy parsing strategy in the models may limit flexibility and lead to suboptimal solutions in some cases.
    *   **Hierarchical assumption:** The assumption of a hierarchical structure in all sequences may not hold in all domains, potentially leading to an overemphasis on certain patterns.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   While chunking and abstraction are established concepts in cognitive science, the dissertation makes a significant contribution by offering a unified computational framework for understanding how these mechanisms interact to support sequence learning. The progression from simple chunking to abstract motif learning is novel, as is the explicit modeling of the trade-off between speed and accuracy. The proposed HVM is a novel model building on HCM and explores concepts in LLMs.
    *   The work has potential implications for understanding human cognition and for developing more efficient machine learning algorithms for sequence processing, pattern recognition, and knowledge representation. The experimental work validates claims that model can be used to infer patterns.
    *   However, the dissertation could benefit from a more thorough discussion of the limitations and potential biases of the generative models, and a stronger connection to real-world applications.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The dissertation demonstrates a solid understanding of the relevant literature, a rigorous approach to research, and a significant contribution to the field of sequence learning and pattern recognition. While limitations remain, including the reliance on simplified sequences and a greedy parsing strategy, the work offers a novel and compelling framework for understanding the interplay of chunking and abstraction in human cognition. The potential for translation to machine learning algorithms further enhances its significance. Thus, the score of 7.5 balances the strengths of the systematic approach and novel models with the acknowledged limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Statistical Impossibility and Possibility of Aligning LLMs with Human Preferences: From Condorcet Paradox to Nash Equilibrium
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10990v1)
- **Authors**: Kaizhao Liu, Qi Long, Zhekun Shi, Weijie J. Su, Jiancong Xiao
- **Abstract**: Aligning large language models (LLMs) with diverse human preferences is critical for ensuring fairness and informed outcomes when deploying these models for decision-making. In this paper, we seek to uncover fundamental statistical limits concerning aligning LLMs with human preferences, with a focus on the probabilistic representation of human preferences and the preservation of diverse preferences in aligned LLMs. We first show that human preferences can be represented by a reward model if and only if the preference among LLM-generated responses is free of any Condorcet cycle. Moreover, we prove that Condorcet cycles exist with probability converging to one exponentially fast under a probabilistic preference model, thereby demonstrating the impossibility of fully aligning human preferences using reward-based approaches such as reinforcement learning from human feedback. Next, we explore the conditions under which LLMs would employ mixed strategies -- meaning they do not collapse to a single response -- when aligned in the limit using a non-reward-based approach, such as Nash learning from human feedback (NLHF). We identify a necessary and sufficient condition for mixed strategies: the absence of a response that is preferred over all others by a majority. As a blessing, we prove that this condition holds with high probability under the probabilistic preference model, thereby highlighting the statistical possibility of preserving minority preferences without explicit regularization in aligning LLMs. Finally, we leverage insights from our statistical results to design a novel, computationally efficient algorithm for finding Nash equilibria in aligning LLMs with NLHF. Our experiments show that Llama-3.2-1B, aligned with our algorithm, achieves a win rate of 60.55\% against the base model.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the statistical limits of aligning Large Language Models (LLMs) with human preferences, focusing on the probabilistic representation of these preferences. It demonstrates that human preferences can be represented by a reward model if and only if there are no Condorcet cycles, and proves the high probability of Condorcet cycles arising under probabilistic preference models, thus showing an impossibility result for reward-based alignment. It then explores the conditions under which LLMs use mixed strategies (preserving diversity) in non-reward-based alignment like Nash Learning from Human Feedback (NLHF), establishing a necessary and sufficient condition based on the absence of a "winning response." It proves this condition holds with high probability under the probabilistic preference model. Finally, it introduces a computationally efficient algorithm, Nash Rejection Sampling (Nash-RS), and shows its effectiveness experimentally with Llama-3.2-1B, achieving an increased win rate against the base model.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a rigorous theoretical framework for analyzing alignment. The connection between Condorcet cycles and reward model limitations is novel and significant. The exploration of mixed strategies and the conditions for diversity preservation in NLHF provides a valuable contribution to the understanding of alternative alignment methods. The Nash-RS algorithm represents a practical algorithmic contribution. However, the use of the impartial culture assumption and the BTL reward model are inherited from the prior work.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem: ensuring fairness and avoiding bias in LLMs aligned with human preferences. The identification of statistical limits and possibilities has important practical implications for LLM alignment. The high probability of Condorcet cycles shows fundamental flaws in reward-based approaches and points to the importance of considering non-reward based approaches. The theoretical results combined with a practical algorithm provide a compelling combination.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Rigorous theoretical analysis.
    *   Clear connection between theoretical results and practical LLM alignment.
    *   The introduction of a new, efficient alignment algorithm.
    *   Solid experimental validation.
    * The extension of work in social choice theory to LLM alignment is interesting.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The Impartial Culture (IC) assumption could be a strong simplification. While the paper argues its validity by stating the broad scope of questions being considered during LLM training, its reliance as the main assumption for multiple proofs undermines its generalizability.
    *   Some of the individual techniques (e.g., rejection sampling) are adapted from existing literature.
    *   Experiments are conducted on a relatively small model (Llama-3.2-1B). It is uncertain how well they translate to larger models.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has a strong potential to influence the field by providing a solid theoretical foundation for understanding and addressing the limitations of current LLM alignment methods and highlighting the possibilities of alternative non-reward based approaches. The Nash-RS algorithm could become a practical tool for improving alignment.

**Overall:** This is a solid contribution that makes significant progress on a vital problem in the field. It brings together formal theory with practical algorithms in a very effective manner. However, it contains a few simplifying assumptions that undermines the practicality of their theoretical results.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.GT
- **Score**: 7/10

### TigerLLM -- A Family of Bangla Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10995v1)
- **Authors**: Nishat Raihan, Marcos Zampieri
- **Abstract**: The development of Large Language Models (LLMs) remains heavily skewed towards English and a few other high-resource languages. This linguistic disparity is particularly evident for Bangla - the 5th most spoken language. A few initiatives attempted to create open-source Bangla LLMs with performance still behind high-resource languages and limited reproducibility. To address this gap, we introduce TigerLLM - a family of Bangla LLMs. Our results demonstrate that these models surpass all open-source alternatives and also outperform larger proprietary models like GPT3.5 across standard benchmarks, establishing TigerLLM as the new baseline for future Bangla language modeling.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TigerLLM - A Family of Bangla Large Language Models".

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TigerLLM, a new family of large language models (LLMs) specifically designed for the Bangla language. The authors address the under-representation of Bangla in the LLM landscape, where open-source models lag behind proprietary counterparts. Their key contributions include: (1) a Bangla-TextBook corpus of curated educational content, (2) a high-quality Bangla-Instruct dataset generated using advanced self-instruct techniques and strong teacher models (GPT-40 and Claude-3.5-Sonnet), and (3) the TigerLLM model family itself, pre-trained and fine-tuned on these datasets. The results show that TigerLLM outperforms existing open-source and even larger proprietary models on various Bangla-specific benchmarks. The paper makes its data and models publicly available to foster further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a significant gap in LLM research by focusing on Bangla, a widely spoken but under-resourced language.  The core strengths lie in the construction of the high-quality Bangla-TextBook and Bangla-Instruct datasets. The use of GPT-40 and Claude-3.5-Sonnet for generating instruction data is a strong point, addressing the limitations of previous approaches that relied on weaker models and machine translation. The paper also demonstrates the effectiveness of their approach through comprehensive benchmark results, showing significant performance gains over existing Bangla LLMs. The thorough documentation and open-sourcing of resources are commendable.

However, there are also areas for critical analysis:

*   **Novelty of Approach:** While the application to Bangla is novel, the underlying techniques (self-instruct, pre-training, fine-tuning) are well-established in the broader LLM field. The novelty lies in the *specific application* and the *careful curation* of data tailored to the Bangla language and culture. How the novel approach used for quality control of the new dataset is something to consider.
*   **Model Size Limitation:** The models are relatively small (1B and 9B parameters). While this enables experimentation and resource efficiency, it potentially limits the ultimate performance achievable compared to larger models. The paper justifies this choice by emphasizing data quality, which is fair, but the potential impact of scaling up the model size with their datasets remains unexplored.
*   **Generalizability:** The focus on educational textbook data, while ensuring quality, may limit the model's performance in other domains (e.g., informal conversation, creative writing). The benchmarks used are a good starting point but could be expanded to cover a wider range of Bangla language tasks. It is also unclear how robust this model is to adversarial attacks or prompts in this language.
*   **Reproducibility:** Although the code and datasets are published, reproducing the results exactly may be challenging due to the dependence on external services like GPT-4 and Claude 3.5, whose APIs and models may change over time. The compute setup is also expensive.

*   **Lack of Analysis:** It is worth noting that the paper's analysis of the model and dataset is quite limited.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by providing a strong baseline for Bangla LLM research. The high-quality datasets and models will likely serve as a foundation for future work in this area. The findings also underscore the importance of data quality over sheer volume, particularly for low-resource languages.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths (high-quality datasets, performance gains, open-sourcing) and weaknesses (model size, generalizability, novelty of techniques), I would assign the paper a score of **7**. The work is significant because it directly addresses the gap of Bangla language LLMs. The careful data construction sets it apart from other similar efforts. However, the incremental novelty of the *approach* and the model size limitations prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the gains in performance are solid, they are achieved using established techniques applied to a new language. There is a significant value in the approach and the data/model releases.

Score: 7
