    *   **Dataset Bias:** Though the effort to use OOD datasets is commendable, the authors could acknowledge the limitations of existing OOD datasets and potential biases that might still be present.
    *   **Resource Intensity** The paper provides limited information about the computational resources required for training and inference, which could be a barrier to adoption for some researchers. Also, the paper depends on proprietary LLMs for some baselines, introducing a lack of reproducibility.
    *   **Lack of ablation with random graph integration:** While the ablation experiments are thorough and demonstrate that multiple strategies contribute to high performance, random integration is an interesting option to include as an experiment.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:
    *   It provides a strong baseline for future work on unified GKG construction.
    *   It encourages the development of more comprehensive and diverse datasets for KG, EKG, and CKG.
    *   It highlights the benefits of curriculum learning and knowledge sharing in graph construction tasks.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

The paper represents a significant contribution to the field by introducing a novel task and demonstrating the feasibility of a unified framework for GKG construction. The comprehensive dataset and well-designed curriculum learning approach are valuable assets. However, the incremental nature of some performance gains, potential dataset biases, the limited evaluation of alternative frameworks, the resource intensity of LLM-based approaches, and the reliance on proprietary models prevent it from achieving a higher score. Overall, the paper has the potential to influence future research and advance the field of knowledge graph construction.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Exploring the Potential of Large Multimodal Models as Effective Alternatives for Pronunciation Assessment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11229v1)
- **Authors**: Ke Wang, Lei He, Kun Liu, Yan Deng, Wenning Wei, Sheng Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) have demonstrated exceptional performance across a wide range of domains. This paper explores their potential in pronunciation assessment tasks, with a particular focus on evaluating the capabilities of the Generative Pre-trained Transformer (GPT) model, specifically GPT-4o. Our study investigates its ability to process speech and audio for pronunciation assessment across multiple levels of granularity and dimensions, with an emphasis on feedback generation and scoring. For our experiments, we use the publicly available Speechocean762 dataset. The evaluation focuses on two key aspects: multi-level scoring and the practicality of the generated feedback. Scoring results are compared against the manual scores provided in the Speechocean762 dataset, while feedback quality is assessed using Large Language Models (LLMs). The findings highlight the effectiveness of integrating LMMs with traditional methods for pronunciation assessment, offering insights into the model's strengths and identifying areas for further improvement.
- **Summary**: ## Paper Summary

The paper explores the use of Large Multimodal Models (LMMs), specifically GPT-4o, for pronunciation assessment (PA) tasks. It evaluates the model's ability to score pronunciation at different granularities (phoneme, word, sentence) and generate helpful feedback for language learners, using the Speechocean762 dataset. The study compares GPT-4o's performance with traditional PA methods, analyzes the quality of generated feedback using other LLMs, and highlights the model's strengths and limitations in the context of PA. The key findings suggest that LMMs can be effective in providing feedback at higher levels of granularity (sentence level) but exhibit weaknesses in scoring at lower levels (phoneme and word level). The paper also proposes integrating LMMs with traditional methods to enhance both feedback quality and scoring accuracy.

## Novelty and Significance Evaluation

The paper presents an interesting and relevant exploration of LMMs for pronunciation assessment.  The novelty lies in directly and systematically evaluating the zero-shot capabilities of GPT-4o, a relatively new and powerful model, across multiple granularities and dimensions in PA, a task traditionally addressed with specialized ASR-based or align-free methods. It also investigates the utility of LLM-generated feedback. However, several aspects limit its significance and novelty:

**Strengths:**

*   **Timeliness:** The research addresses a current and rapidly developing area of LLMs and their application to education, specifically CALL.
*   **Direct Evaluation:** Directly evaluating GPT-4o on a publicly available pronunciation dataset provides valuable insights and a benchmark for future research.
*   **Multi-Granularity Approach:**  Examining performance across different levels of granularity (phoneme, word, sentence) is valuable as it highlights the model's strengths and weaknesses at different levels of analysis and provides insights into specific areas needing improvement.
*   **Feedback Assessment:**  The attempt to automatically evaluate the feedback generated by the LLMs is a positive step, although, as noted below, this is an area requiring more robust methods.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Methodology:** The reliance on purely zero-shot prompting and a single dataset limits the generalizability of the findings.  While justified by limitations of the API, the one-shot experiments' poor results reported diminish this justification.  Exploring different prompting strategies or few-shot learning, even with a limited subset of data, could have strengthened the analysis.
*   **Lack of Fine-tuning:** The absence of fine-tuning on domain-specific data is a significant limitation. PA is a specialized task, and fine-tuning could significantly improve performance. It is expected that performance will increase significantly with fine-tuning so the zero-shot results here might not be that interesting.
*   **Weak Performance:** The results show a significant performance gap between GPT-4o and traditional models, particularly in scoring accuracy. The high failure rate (unscored outcomes) in both single and multi-granularity tasks also raises concerns about the model's reliability and consistency. A deep dive into the cases where GPT-4 failed to provide an answer would have been insightful.
*   **Subjective Feedback Evaluation:** Using other LLMs to evaluate feedback quality, while practical given the lack of ground truth, is inherently subjective and raises questions about the validity of the assessment. Further, relying solely on "helpfulness" and "correlation" scores may not fully capture the nuances of effective feedback, especially in PA. A deeper qualitative assessment of feedback content and alignment with scoring errors would be more compelling.
*   **Incremental Contribution:** Compared to recent work like [25] which utilizes multimodal LMMs for PA through acoustic encoding, the novelty in this study is less significant, particularly as the performance achieved falls short of the referenced study.
*   **Comparison Baseline:** Azure PA is a useful comparison, but the lack of phoneme comparison due to different datasets reduces the credibility of their results.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance is somewhat limited due to the aforementioned weaknesses. While it highlights the potential of LMMs in PA, the current performance of GPT-4o without fine-tuning doesn't demonstrate a clear advantage over existing methods. The study primarily serves as an initial exploration and benchmark, identifying areas where LMMs can be improved for PA tasks. The observation regarding the high failure rate is important in highlighting where additional research effort might be needed.

**Justification for the Score:**

Given the above analysis, the paper presents a valuable initial exploration of LMMs for PA but suffers from several limitations that weaken its overall novelty and significance.  While the multi-granularity approach and feedback assessment are positive aspects, the weak performance of GPT-4o without fine-tuning, limited methodological choices, and the reliance on LLMs for feedback evaluation prevent it from being a groundbreaking contribution.

Score: 5

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 5/10

### PrivacyScalpel: Enhancing LLM Privacy via Interpretable Feature Intervention with Sparse Autoencoders
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11232v1)
- **Authors**: Ahmed Frikha, Muhammad Reza Ar Razi, Krishna Kanth Nakka, Ricardo Mendes, Xue Jiang, Xuebing Zhou
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in natural language processing but also pose significant privacy risks by memorizing and leaking Personally Identifiable Information (PII). Existing mitigation strategies, such as differential privacy and neuron-level interventions, often degrade model utility or fail to effectively prevent leakage. To address this challenge, we introduce PrivacyScalpel, a novel privacy-preserving framework that leverages LLM interpretability techniques to identify and mitigate PII leakage while maintaining performance. PrivacyScalpel comprises three key steps: (1) Feature Probing, which identifies layers in the model that encode PII-rich representations, (2) Sparse Autoencoding, where a k-Sparse Autoencoder (k-SAE) disentangles and isolates privacy-sensitive features, and (3) Feature-Level Interventions, which employ targeted ablation and vector steering to suppress PII leakage. Our empirical evaluation on Gemma2-2b and Llama2-7b, fine-tuned on the Enron dataset, shows that PrivacyScalpel significantly reduces email leakage from 5.15\% to as low as 0.0\%, while maintaining over 99.4\% of the original model's utility. Notably, our method outperforms neuron-level interventions in privacy-utility trade-offs, demonstrating that acting on sparse, monosemantic features is more effective than manipulating polysemantic neurons. Beyond improving LLM privacy, our approach offers insights into the mechanisms underlying PII memorization, contributing to the broader field of model interpretability and secure AI deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the PrivacyScalpel paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PrivacyScalpel, a novel framework designed to enhance the privacy of Large Language Models (LLMs) by mitigating the leakage of Personally Identifiable Information (PII).  PrivacyScalpel leverages LLM interpretability techniques, specifically sparse autoencoders (SAEs), to identify and isolate privacy-sensitive features within the model's activations. The framework then employs feature-level interventions, including targeted ablation and vector steering, to suppress PII leakage while preserving the model's utility. The authors evaluate PrivacyScalpel on fine-tuned Gemma2-2b and Llama2-7b models using the Enron dataset, demonstrating significant reductions in email leakage with minimal performance degradation compared to neuron-level intervention methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a somewhat novel approach by combining interpretability techniques (SAEs) with feature-level interventions for privacy preservation in LLMs. While neuron-level interventions exist, operating on disentangled features identified by SAEs adds a valuable layer of control and interpretability, potentially leading to better privacy-utility tradeoffs. Feature-level intervention is not *entirely* new, as others have explored similar ideas but not precisely with the combination of SAEs and these specific intervention techniques. Therefore, while it builds upon existing work, the combination is significant enough to consider it moderately novel. The use of "probing" to identify the most sensitive layers is also not novel in itself, but a standard method.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the growing awareness of privacy risks associated with LLMs.  The paper contributes a practical framework that addresses these concerns without significantly sacrificing model performance. The improvement compared to neuron-level interventions is compelling. Furthermore, by providing insights into the mechanisms of PII memorization within LLMs through the interpretability lens of SAEs, the research contributes to a better understanding of the problem, which is important for future defense mechanisms. Providing insights into how PII is being memorized is certainly valuable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem definition and motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the privacy challenges in LLMs and the need for effective mitigation strategies.
    *   **Well-defined framework:** PrivacyScalpel is presented as a coherent and well-structured framework with clear steps and components.
    *   **Comprehensive experimental evaluation:** The authors conduct experiments on different models (Gemma and Llama2) and datasets, demonstrating the robustness of their approach. They also compared against neuron-level intervention which adds strength.
    *   **Interpretability-driven insights:** The paper connects the effectiveness of its approach to the interpretability afforded by SAEs, providing valuable insights into how LLMs memorize and leak PII.
    *   The ablations and varying parameters of Steering and Probing are helpful in solidifying the best practices.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited scope of PII:** The experiments focus primarily on email addresses. While important, exploring other forms of PII (e.g., phone numbers, financial data) would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Dataset Size effect is only explored minimally:** They show a slight difference on some defense strategies when the amount of training data is changed from 100% to 1%, however, this is just one datapoint on a spectrum of data percentages.
    *   **Potential for adversarial attacks on SAEs:** The reliance on SAEs introduces potential vulnerabilities. Clever adversarial attacks targeting the SAEs could potentially bypass the privacy protections.
    *   **Computational cost of SAE training:** Training SAEs can be computationally expensive, potentially limiting the scalability of PrivacyScalpel for very large LLMs or real-time applications.
    *   **Reliance on Enron dataset:** The Enron dataset, while containing real-world email data, may not be representative of all types of sensitive information or use cases.
    *   The "probing" step is fairly straightforward, and might not be the ideal layer in complex scenarios.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The work has the potential to influence future research on privacy-preserving LLMs by highlighting the benefits of feature-level interventions and the importance of interpretability in understanding and mitigating privacy risks.
    *   The proposed framework could be adopted by practitioners developing privacy-sensitive applications of LLMs, providing a practical approach to enhancing model privacy.
    *   The insights into PII memorization mechanisms could contribute to the development of more robust and inherently privacy-aware LLM architectures.

* **Overall:**

PrivacyScalpel presents a useful combination of existing methods for privacy enhancement, which achieves a compelling balance between privacy preservation and model utility, surpassing neuron-level interventions. However, limitations in scope, data reliance, potential attacks, and computational burden temper the potential for revolutionary impact. Therefore:

**Score: 7**

The framework offers a practically significant step forward for privacy-preserving LLMs by thoughtfully leveraging insights and techniques from model interpretability. While several aspects require further refinement and comprehensive validation across diverse scenarios, the work delivers a novel and effective method with clear potential for real-world deployment.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Addressing Information Loss and Interaction Collapse: A Dual Enhanced Attention Framework for Feature Interaction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11233v1)
- **Authors**: Yi Xu, Zhiyuan Lu, Xiaochen Li, Jinxin Hu, Hong Wen, Zulong Chen, Yu Zhang, Jing Zhang
- **Abstract**: The Transformer has proven to be a significant approach in feature interaction for CTR prediction, achieving considerable success in previous works. However, it also presents potential challenges in handling feature interactions. Firstly, Transformers may encounter information loss when capturing feature interactions. By relying on inner products to represent pairwise relationships, they compress raw interaction information, which can result in a degradation of fidelity. Secondly, due to the long-tail features distribution, feature fields with low information-abundance embeddings constrain the information abundance of other fields, leading to collapsed embedding matrices. To tackle these issues, we propose a Dual Attention Framework for Enhanced Feature Interaction, known as Dual Enhanced Attention. This framework integrates two attention mechanisms: the Combo-ID attention mechanism and the collapse-avoiding attention mechanism. The Combo-ID attention mechanism directly retains feature interaction pairs to mitigate information loss, while the collapse-avoiding attention mechanism adaptively filters out low information-abundance interaction pairs to prevent interaction collapse. Extensive experiments conducted on industrial datasets have shown the effectiveness of Dual Enhanced Attention.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses two key challenges in using Transformers for click-through rate (CTR) prediction: information loss during feature interaction and interaction collapse due to long-tail feature distributions.  The authors propose a Dual Enhanced Attention framework, "Dual Enhanced Attention," which integrates two attention mechanisms: Combo-ID attention (to retain feature interaction pairs and mitigate information loss) and collapse-avoiding attention (to filter out low-information interaction pairs and prevent collapse). They perform experiments on industrial datasets and demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a dual attention mechanism to address specific challenges faced by Transformers in CTR prediction tasks. While individual components (using ID combinations or dynamic thresholding) have similarities with prior works in feature interaction, the combination of these ideas within a Transformer architecture to address both information loss and interaction collapse demonstrates a level of novelty. The 'Gated Siamese Codebook' approach for mitigating hash collision issues also introduces a unique solution within the Combo-ID attention mechanism.

*   **Significance:** The problems addressed are significant within the context of large-scale recommender systems. Information loss and interaction collapse can significantly impact the performance of CTR prediction models. The reported improvements in GAUC on industrial datasets suggest that the proposed method offers practical value. The careful ablation studies help to understand the contribution of individual components, making it valuable for other researchers looking to build upon this work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly identifies and articulates the problem of information loss and interaction collapse in Transformers for CTR prediction.
    *   The proposed solution, Dual Enhanced Attention, is well-motivated and technically sound.
    *   The use of two distinct attention mechanisms to address separate aspects of the problem is a strong architectural design choice.
    *   The ablation studies provide a detailed understanding of the effectiveness of each component.
    *   The experiments are conducted on large-scale industrial datasets, increasing the credibility and applicability of the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth comparison to existing methods that tackle similar problems. For example, the MemoNet is mentioned as a multi-hash codebook but the differences could be elaborated on more.
    *   While the experimental results are positive, the improvements in GAUC, while appreciable, are relatively modest.
    *   The paper's writing and organization, while understandable, can be improved to clearly specify and explain the motivation for each design decision.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in CTR prediction, particularly in the context of Transformer-based models. The insights into information loss and interaction collapse are valuable for researchers working on similar problems. The proposed Dual Enhanced Attention framework can serve as a starting point for developing more advanced feature interaction mechanisms.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution with a novel approach. The improvements over baselines, although not groundbreaking, demonstrate the value of the proposed methods. The clear definition of the problem and the well-designed experiments are strengths. The weaknesses in comparison to other existing methods and less improvements reduce the score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Better Alignment: Training Diffusion Models with Reinforcement Learning Against Sparse Rewards
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11240v1)
- **Authors**: Zijing Hu, Fengda Zhang, Long Chen, Kun Kuang, Jiahui Li, Kaifeng Gao, Jun Xiao, Xin Wang, Wenwu Zhu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have achieved remarkable success in text-to-image generation. However, their practical applications are hindered by the misalignment between generated images and corresponding text prompts. To tackle this issue, reinforcement learning (RL) has been considered for diffusion model fine-tuning. Yet, RL's effectiveness is limited by the challenge of sparse reward, where feedback is only available at the end of the generation process. This makes it difficult to identify which actions during the denoising process contribute positively to the final generated image, potentially leading to ineffective or unnecessary denoising policies. To this end, this paper presents a novel RL-based framework that addresses the sparse reward problem when training diffusion models. Our framework, named $\text{B}^2\text{-DiffuRL}$, employs two strategies: \textbf{B}ackward progressive training and \textbf{B}ranch-based sampling. For one thing, backward progressive training focuses initially on the final timesteps of denoising process and gradually extends the training interval to earlier timesteps, easing the learning difficulty from sparse rewards. For another, we perform branch-based sampling for each training interval. By comparing the samples within the same branch, we can identify how much the policies of the current training interval contribute to the final image, which helps to learn effective policies instead of unnecessary ones. $\text{B}^2\text{-DiffuRL}$ is compatible with existing optimization algorithms. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of $\text{B}^2\text{-DiffuRL}$ in improving prompt-image alignment and maintaining diversity in generated images. The code for this work is available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Towards Better Alignment: Training Diffusion Models with Reinforcement Learning Against Sparse Rewards" tackles the problem of misalignment between generated images and text prompts in diffusion models.  It addresses the challenge of sparse rewards in reinforcement learning (RL) based fine-tuning, where feedback is only available at the end of the generation process, making it difficult to identify which actions during denoising contribute positively.  The authors propose B2-DiffuRL, a framework with two key strategies: (1) Backward Progressive Training (BPT), which focuses on training the model on the final timesteps first and then gradually extending the training interval, and (2) Branch-based Sampling (BS), which involves comparing samples within the same branch to identify the contribution of policies in the current training interval.  They demonstrate that B2-DiffuRL improves prompt-image alignment while maintaining image diversity and is compatible with existing optimization algorithms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a novel framework, B2-DiffuRL, that combines two relatively straightforward strategies (BPT and BS) to address a well-known problem in RL-based diffusion model training. While neither BPT nor BS is entirely revolutionary on its own, their combination and application to the specific context of sparse rewards in diffusion models represent a contribution. The analysis of the sparse reward problem in this domain is also valuable.

**Significance:** The misalignment problem in text-to-image diffusion models is a significant barrier to their practical application.  By addressing the sparse reward challenge, the paper offers a practical approach to improve the controllability and reliability of these models.  The reported improvements in prompt-image alignment and the preservation of diversity are meaningful.  The framework's compatibility with existing optimization algorithms makes it readily adoptable by the research community. The paper identifies a key bottleneck in RL finetuning diffusion models and provides a viable solution that future research can build upon.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of sparse rewards in RL-based diffusion model training and its impact on alignment and diversity.
*   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The proposed B2-DiffuRL framework is well-motivated by the challenges of sparse rewards and offers intuitive solutions.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides thorough experimental results, including comparisons with state-of-the-art methods (DDPO) and ablation studies to demonstrate the effectiveness of the individual components. The inclusion of human preference tests strengthens the evaluation.
*   **Compatibility:** The framework's compatibility with existing optimization algorithms enhances its practicality.
*   **Addresses a core need**: The paper recognizes the need to finetune diffusion models so they are better aligned with the prompt, and that this finetuning could lead to a loss of diversity.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of BPT and BS is novel in this context, the individual techniques themselves are not groundbreaking. The novelty stems from the application and analysis within the diffusion model domain.
*   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The paper lacks a deep theoretical analysis of why BPT and BS are effective in addressing the sparse reward problem. A more rigorous analysis would strengthen the claims. The paper would benefit from a tighter discussion on the choice of hyperparameter T, particularly relating this value to some property of either the prompt or the images.
*   **Dependency on existing methods**: B2-DiffuRL needs existing RL models for its operation (DDPO, DPO, etc). It does not stand alone and is completely reliant on the performance of those models.
*   **Limited Generalization tests**: Generalization is only shown on prompts that follow the same grammatical structure as the trained prompts (although with different vocabulary). It would have been interesting to see a transfer of the training to more complex prompts.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of RL-based fine-tuning of diffusion models. The B2-DiffuRL framework offers a practical and effective approach to address the sparse reward problem, and the insights provided by the paper can guide future research in this area. It is likely to be a valuable reference for researchers working on improving the controllability and reliability of text-to-image diffusion models.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing the sparse reward problem in RL-based diffusion model training. While the proposed techniques are not radically novel, their combination and application to this specific context are well-motivated and empirically validated. The paper's strengths include a clear problem definition, a well-motivated approach, and thorough experimental results. The main weaknesses are the incremental nature of the novelty, the limited theoretical analysis, the dependence on existing RL algorithms, and the limited nature of the generalization tests. Although the lack of complex generalization tests harms the results, B2-DiffuRL addresses a well-identified problem and could have a significant impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLMPerf: GPU Performance Modeling meets Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11244v1)
- **Authors**: Khoi N. M. Nguyen, Hoang Duy Nguyen Do, Huyen Thao Le, Thanh Tuan Dao
- **Abstract**: Performance modeling, a pivotal domain in program cost analysis, currently relies on manually crafted models constrained by various program and hardware limitations, especially in the intricate landscape of GPGPU. Meanwhile, Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated their effectiveness in addressing diverse programming challenges. Our work establishes a connection between LLMs and performance modeling, employing the LLM as a performance estimator. Through experimental exploration with carefully designed large-scale OpenCL datasets, we highlight the potential capability as well as the main difficulties of using LLMs in handling performance modeling tasks for OpenCL device source programs. As the first study for this line of work, our LLM-based performance model achieves a mean absolute percentage error of $24.25\%$ for a large-scale generated validation set. On a set of publicly available OpenCL programs, our model achieves a mean absolute percentage error of $46.1\%$.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "LLMPerf: GPU Performance Modeling meets Large Language Models" explores the potential of using Large Language Models (LLMs) for GPU performance modeling, specifically for OpenCL kernels. The paper introduces a framework for automatically collecting execution time data for GPU kernels, generating a large-scale OpenCL performance dataset.  The authors fine-tune the CodeGen LLM family to predict the execution time of OpenCL kernels based solely on static code analysis, including source code, kernel input, and execution settings. They evaluate their LLM-based performance model (LLMPerf) on a generated dataset and established OpenCL benchmarks (SHOC and Rodinia), demonstrating the model's capabilities and limitations. The paper also discusses data enrichment techniques and potential future research directions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of applying LLMs to GPU performance modeling is **somewhat novel**, although LLMs have been used for code-related tasks before. Generating a large-scale OpenCL dataset, while practically useful, is not inherently groundbreaking. The combination of these two, however, does present a unique approach. Data augmentation using a combination of compiler memory analysis techniques (Clang AST instrumentation) with IQR outlier handling constitutes a significant addition that is worth noting.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to automate and simplify GPU performance modeling, a traditionally challenging task requiring expert knowledge and significant manual effort. If successful, this approach could be integrated into automated program optimization and resource allocation tools. However, the current accuracy levels (MAPEs of 24.25% on the generated dataset and 46.11% on real benchmarks) suggest that the model is still in its early stages of development.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Automated Dataset Generation:** The framework for generating large-scale OpenCL performance datasets is a valuable contribution, as it addresses the scarcity of publicly available data.
    *   **Static Analysis Approach:** The use of static code analysis, without relying on runtime information, makes the model more practical and widely applicable.
    *   **LLM-Based Approach:** Exploring the use of LLMs for performance modeling offers a potentially more scalable and adaptable approach compared to traditional analytical or statistical methods.
    *   **Thorough Evaluation:** The evaluation is conducted on both a generated dataset and established OpenCL benchmarks, providing a comprehensive assessment of the model's performance.
    *   **Discussion of Limitations:** The authors transparently discuss the limitations of their approach, which includes dependency on static correlations between sizes and other data.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Accuracy:** The accuracy of the model, particularly on real benchmarks, needs improvement. A MAPE of 46.11% is relatively high and limits the practical applicability of the model.
    *   **Data Dependence:** The model is likely highly dependent on the specific characteristics of the training dataset. It is important to determine how robust the model is to changes in GPU architecture and programming styles.
    *   **Lack of Comparative Analysis:** The paper lacks a direct comparison to existing GPU performance modeling techniques. This omission makes it difficult to assess the relative merits of the LLM-based approach.
    *   **Limited Context Length Exploitation:** While the use of LLMs is promising, the paper doesn't fully explore advanced LLM capabilities such as few-shot learning, transfer learning from other code-related tasks, or prompt engineering to provide richer context to the model.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research in GPU performance modeling, particularly in the application of LLMs. The generated dataset and the proposed LLM-based approach could serve as a starting point for further investigation and improvement. The methodology used for data generation, particularly the combination of compiler analysis with IQR and memory usage information, is worth further exploration. However, the current limitations in accuracy may hinder its immediate adoption in practical applications.

*   **Justification of Score:** The score is based on the paper's novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses. While the idea of using LLMs for GPU performance modeling is interesting, the current accuracy levels and the lack of comparative analysis limit its immediate impact. The generated dataset and automated framework are valuable contributions, but the overall novelty and significance are not high enough to warrant a higher score. This paper is a solid first step in this area, but requires further refinement and validation to achieve its full potential.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.PF
- **Score**: 6/10

### Noise Synthesis for Low-Light Image Denoising with Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11262v1)
- **Authors**: Liying Lu, Raphaël Achddou, Sabine Süsstrunk
- **Abstract**: Low-light photography produces images with low signal-to-noise ratios due to limited photons. In such conditions, common approximations like the Gaussian noise model fall short, and many denoising techniques fail to remove noise effectively. Although deep-learning methods perform well, they require large datasets of paired images that are impractical to acquire. As a remedy, synthesizing realistic low-light noise has gained significant attention. In this paper, we investigate the ability of diffusion models to capture the complex distribution of low-light noise. We show that a naive application of conventional diffusion models is inadequate for this task and propose three key adaptations that enable high-precision noise generation without calibration or post-processing: a two-branch architecture to better model signal-dependent and signal-independent noise, the incorporation of positional information to capture fixed-pattern noise, and a tailored diffusion noise schedule. Consequently, our model enables the generation of large datasets for training low-light denoising networks, leading to state-of-the-art performance. Through comprehensive analysis, including statistical evaluation and noise decomposition, we provide deeper insights into the characteristics of the generated data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of synthesizing realistic noise for training low-light image denoising networks.  Recognizing that simple noise models like Gaussian noise are inadequate and that acquiring real paired noisy-clean image data is challenging, the authors propose using diffusion models to learn and generate complex low-light noise distributions.  They identify that naive application of diffusion models is insufficient and introduce three key adaptations: a two-branch architecture to model signal-dependent and independent noise separately, positional encoding to capture fixed-pattern noise, and a tailored diffusion noise schedule to preserve variance.  Experiments demonstrate that denoising networks trained on data synthesized with their approach achieve state-of-the-art performance compared to other noise synthesis techniques and even models trained on real data.  The paper provides in-depth analysis of the generated noise through statistical evaluation and decomposition.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using diffusion models for noise synthesis is interesting, but its real impact relies on the tailored adaptations the authors propose. The **most novel aspect is the combination of the two-branch architecture with positional encoding and the careful selection of the diffusion noise schedule.** While the components themselves aren't necessarily entirely new individually, their combination and application within the noise synthesis context is a significant and well-motivated. Previous work has explored GANs and normalizing flows for noise modelling, making the use of diffusion models a relevant exploration, yet the naive application reveals its inadequacy, forcing the authors to be inventive.

*   **Significance:** The problem of generating realistic noise for low-light image denoising is vital, especially given the difficulty of acquiring paired data.  The paper presents a significant advancement by showing the feasibility of using diffusion models, when properly adapted, to address this problem. **The results showing improved denoising performance compared to other synthesis methods and even real data are compelling.** This reduces reliance on labor-intensive paired data collection.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive analysis:** The paper provides thorough statistical analysis and noise decomposition, offering deep insights into the characteristics of the generated data and justifying the design choices made.
    *   **Strong results:** The experimental results demonstrate state-of-the-art performance in denoising, a tangible benefit of their improved noise synthesis technique.
    *   **Well-motivated adaptations:** The paper clearly explains the shortcomings of applying diffusion models directly and logically motivates the proposed adaptations to address them.
    *   The paper is well-written and the approach clearly detailed, with appropriate references.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Generalization:** While the SID and ELD datasets are well-known, their limited diversity and narrow camera-setting configurations, potentially limits the wider applicability of the work. The authors acknowledge the generalization limitations to unseen camera settings, which is a weakness. However, the decomposition and understanding of noise patterns provide a foundation that might enable adaptation to new cameras by adjusting the conditioning embeddings.
    *   **Computational cost:** Diffusion models can be computationally expensive to train and sample from. While the authors use DDIM to mitigate sampling costs, more efficient alternatives would enhance practicality.
    *   **Limited scope of applications:** This work is narrowly focused on low-light image denoising, and it might have broader implications for general noise modeling for image processing.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by enabling the generation of large, high-quality training datasets for low-light image denoising.  This could lead to improvements in denoising performance across a range of devices and applications. The work also provides a blueprint for adapting diffusion models to other noise modeling tasks.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the combination of novelty, significance, strong results, and the clearly-articulated and analyzed method, along with the limitations identified, a score of **7.5** is justified. While diffusion models are becoming popular, this work provides important insights on making them effective for low-light noise synthesis and highlights the role of specific architectural adaptations.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 0/10

### CyclePose -- Leveraging Cycle-Consistency for Annotation-Free Nuclei Segmentation in Fluorescence Microscopy
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11266v1)
- **Authors**: Jonas Utz, Stefan Vocht, Anne Tjorven Buessen, Dennis Possart, Fabian Wagner, Mareike Thies, Mingxuan Gu, Stefan Uderhardt, Katharina Breininger
- **Abstract**: In recent years, numerous neural network architectures specifically designed for the instance segmentation of nuclei in microscopic images have been released. These models embed nuclei-specific priors to outperform generic architectures like U-Nets; however, they require large annotated datasets, which are often not available. Generative models (GANs, diffusion models) have been used to compensate for this by synthesizing training data. These two-stage approaches are computationally expensive, as first a generative model and then a segmentation model has to be trained. We propose CyclePose, a hybrid framework integrating synthetic data generation and segmentation training. CyclePose builds on a CycleGAN architecture, which allows unpaired translation between microscopy images and segmentation masks. We embed a segmentation model into CycleGAN and leverage a cycle consistency loss for self-supervision. Without annotated data, CyclePose outperforms other weakly or unsupervised methods on two public datasets. Code is available at https://github.com/jonasutz/CyclePose
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CyclePose, an unsupervised framework for nuclei segmentation in fluorescence microscopy images.  It leverages a CycleGAN architecture to translate between synthetic segmentation masks and microscopy images.  A key innovation is the integration of a Cellpose-based segmentation model directly into the CycleGAN loop. The cycle consistency loss provides a self-supervisory signal, eliminating the need for annotated training data.  The method is enhanced with a Perlin noise-based texture generation and a mask-to-image loss to improve segmentation accuracy and realism of generated images. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of CyclePose on two public datasets, showing it outperforms other unsupervised methods and achieves performance comparable to some supervised approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The integration of a pre-trained segmentation model (Cellpose) into a CycleGAN framework for unsupervised training is a significant contribution. Previous methods often rely on either two-step approaches (generating synthetic data then training a segmentation model) or direct image-to-image translation that may not be well-suited for segmentation tasks.  The use of cycle consistency loss for self-supervision in this context is a clever idea. While using CycleGANs for unsupervised learning and segmentation isn't entirely new, directly embedding a segmentation network within the CycleGAN is a novel architectural choice. The added losses, especially the mask-to-image loss, contributes to the robustness.

*   **Significance:**  The ability to perform accurate nuclei segmentation without labeled data is highly valuable, as manual annotation is a significant bottleneck in biomedical image analysis.  CyclePose has the potential to reduce annotation costs and enable broader application of deep learning to microscopy data, particularly when training data is limited. The results show impressive improvements over existing unsupervised approaches on the benchmark datasets. However, the performance gap between CyclePose and fully supervised Cellpose still exists, indicating room for further improvement. The ablation studies clearly demonstrate the positive impact of each component of the framework, especially the Perlin and the mask-to-image losses.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Annotation-Free:**  Eliminates the need for labor-intensive manual annotations.
    *   **Direct Integration:** Combines data generation and segmentation training in a single framework.
    *   **Competitive Performance:** Achieves state-of-the-art unsupervised segmentation results.
    *   **Well-Designed Architecture:**  The integration of Cellpose into CycleGAN is well-motivated.
    *   **Clear Ablation Studies:**  Demonstrates the contributions of different loss functions.
    *   **Good Experimental Design:** Utilizes standard datasets and metrics to assess performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Synthetic Masks:** The method still depends on creating synthetic masks based on simple ellipses, which might not perfectly represent the diversity of real-world nuclei shapes.
    *   **Model Selection Requires Labeled Data:** A small subset of annotated data is used for model selection, somewhat diminishing the "completely unsupervised" claim, though the amount of annotated data required is significantly less.
    *   **Potential for Improvement:** While the performance is good, the gap with fully supervised Cellpose indicates room for improvement. More advanced texture synthesis or shape priors might further boost the results.
    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation** The evaluation is on two datasets; expanding it to more diverse datasets is necessary.
    *   **Generalization Issues:** The method might struggle to generalize to new cell types or imaging modalities if the synthetic mask generation process isn't adapted accordingly.
    *   **Code Release is crucial:** The utility and impact depend on the accessibility of the code and its usability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's approach can likely be extended to other segmentation problems in medical imaging where labeled data is scarce. The core idea of integrating a segmentation network within a CycleGAN and using cycle consistency for self-supervision can inspire new unsupervised learning methods. It provides a foundation for further research into domain adaptation and synthetic data generation for biomedical image analysis.

Score: 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and effective approach to unsupervised nuclei segmentation, addressing a critical need in the field. The results are impressive and demonstrate the potential of the method. However, the reliance on synthetic masks, the use of a small labeled dataset for model selection, and the performance gap with supervised approaches limit the overall impact. While the technique has clear benefits and is a valuable contribution, it is not a groundbreaking advancement that completely revolutionizes the field. This score reflects the paper's significant contribution, while acknowledging the limitations and areas for future improvement. The critical factor to increase the impact would be greater generalizability without requiring any labelled data.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### When Do Transformers Outperform Feedforward and Recurrent Networks? A Statistical Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11272v1)
- **Authors**: Alireza Mousavi-Hosseini, Clayton Sanford, Denny Wu, Murat A. Erdogdu
- **Abstract**: Theoretical efforts to prove advantages of Transformers in comparison with classical architectures such as feedforward and recurrent neural networks have mostly focused on representational power. In this work, we take an alternative perspective and prove that even with infinite compute, feedforward and recurrent networks may suffer from larger sample complexity compared to Transformers, as the latter can adapt to a form of dynamic sparsity. Specifically, we consider a sequence-to-sequence data generating model on sequences of length $N$, in which the output at each position depends only on $q$ relevant tokens with $q \ll N$, and the positions of these tokens are described in the input prompt. We prove that a single-layer Transformer can learn this model if and only if its number of attention heads is at least $q$, in which case it achieves a sample complexity almost independent of $N$, while recurrent networks require $N^{\Omega(1)}$ samples on the same problem. If we simplify this model, recurrent networks may achieve a complexity almost independent of $N$, while feedforward networks still require $N$ samples. Consequently, our proposed sparse retrieval model illustrates a natural hierarchy in sample complexity across these architectures.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a theoretical analysis comparing the sample complexity of Transformers, recurrent neural networks (RNNs), and feedforward networks (FFNs) on a sequence-to-sequence learning problem called q-Sparse Token Regression (qSTR). In qSTR, the output at each position depends on a small, dynamic subset (size *q*) of the input tokens, with the relevant tokens indicated in the input prompt. The authors prove that single-layer Transformers can learn qSTR with a sample complexity nearly independent of the input sequence length *N* (assuming enough attention heads), while RNNs require Ω(*N*^c) samples, and FFNs require Ω(*N*) samples. The paper highlights a statistical separation, showing that Transformers can learn this dynamic sparsity structure more efficiently, even with infinite computational resources for the other architectures. They also analyze a simpler version of qSTR where RNNs can achieve logarithmic sample complexity, while FFNs still need linear. Experimental results validate the theoretical findings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in its focus on *statistical* separation between architectures, contrasting with prior work that primarily focuses on representational power or approximation error.  The qSTR model is a conceptually simple yet effective way to capture dynamic sparsity, a relevant attribute of many real-world sequence tasks. The rigorous lower bounds for RNNs and FFNs are also novel, especially the lower bound technique developed for FFNs to get a sample complexity linear in N. While the general idea of sparsity is not new, its articulation in this dynamic, sequence-dependent retrieval setting is innovative. The lower bound technique for RNNs, which leverages entropy-based arguments, is also a novel addition.

**Significance:** The paper's significance is moderate. It addresses a fundamental question of when and why Transformers excel over other architectures. Understanding the role of dynamic sparsity in the Transformer's success can guide the design of more efficient architectures or training strategies. The theoretical findings provide insights into the inductive biases of each architecture, specifically highlighting the Transformer's inherent ability to adapt to dynamic sparsity. The analysis provides a foundation for future investigations into length generalization, which is a crucial aspect of sequence learning.

**Strengths:**

*   **Rigorous Theoretical Analysis:** The paper provides clear definitions, assumptions, and theorems, along with detailed proofs.
*   **Clear Separation Results:** The theoretical analysis convincingly demonstrates the sample complexity advantage of Transformers for the qSTR model.
*   **Relevant Model:** The qSTR model captures an important aspect of sequence learning with dynamic sparsity, making the results practically relevant.
*   **Novel Techniques:** The paper develops new lower bound techniques for both FFNs and RNNs which are valuable contributions in themselves.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The experiments, though simple, support the theoretical findings and strengthen the paper's conclusions.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Simplifications and Assumptions:** The qSTR model is still a simplified representation of real-world tasks. Strong assumptions are made about the link function (Assumption 2), and the data distribution (Assumption 1). These assumptions may limit the generalizability of the results. More discussion about the tightness of the assumptions could help.
*   **Single-Layer Transformer:** The analysis focuses on single-layer Transformers, which are not representative of the deep Transformers used in practice. While analyzing a single-layer network makes the analysis tractable, its relevance could be questioned.
*   **Limited Experimental Scope:** The experiments are limited in scope, using simple synthetic data.  Experiments on more realistic datasets would further validate the theoretical findings.
*   **Practical Applicability:**  While theoretically interesting, the concrete implications for practitioners on how to improve training and architectures are not explicitly discussed.

**Overall:** The paper makes a solid contribution to the theoretical understanding of Transformers. While the model is simplified and the experimental scope is limited, the theoretical analysis is rigorous and provides valuable insights into the statistical advantages of Transformers. The development of new lower bound techniques is an additional strength. The paper is worthy of publication and should stimulate further research in this area.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**  The paper has good novelty and is theoretically sound, demonstrating a clear separation between architectures on a specific problem. However, the reliance on simplifying assumptions and the limited experimental validation hold it back from a higher score. The theoretical results are interesting and significant, but the practical implications and the model’s limitations could be better addressed.

- **Classification**: stat.ML
- **Score**: 6/10

### High-Dimensional Interlingual Representations of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11280v1)
- **Authors**: Bryan Wilie, Samuel Cahyawijaya, Junxian He, Pascale Fung
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) trained on massive multilingual datasets hint at the formation of interlingual constructs--a shared subspace in the representation space. However, evidence regarding this phenomenon is mixed, leaving it unclear whether these models truly develop unified interlingual representations, or present a partially aligned constructs. We explore 31 diverse languages varying on their resource-levels, typologies, and geographical regions; and find that multilingual LLMs exhibit inconsistent cross-lingual alignments. To address this, we propose an interlingual representation framework identifying both the shared interlingual semantic subspace and fragmented components, existed due to representational limitations. We introduce Interlingual Local Overlap (ILO) score to quantify interlingual alignment by comparing the local neighborhood structures of high-dimensional representations. We utilize ILO to investigate the impact of single-language fine-tuning on the interlingual representations in multilingual LLMs. Our results indicate that training exclusively on a single language disrupts the alignment in early layers, while freezing these layers preserves the alignment of interlingual representations, leading to improved cross-lingual generalization. These results validate our framework and metric for evaluating interlingual representation, and further underscore that interlingual alignment is crucial for scalable multilingual learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates interlingual representations in large language models (LLMs). It questions whether multilingual LLMs develop truly unified interlingual representations or if they only present partially aligned constructs. The authors explore 31 diverse languages and find inconsistent cross-lingual alignments. To address this, they propose a framework identifying both the shared interlingual semantic subspace and fragmented components due to representational limitations. They introduce Interlingual Local Overlap (ILO) score to quantify interlingual alignment by comparing local neighborhood structures in high-dimensional representations. The authors utilize ILO to analyze the impact of single-language fine-tuning and observe that it disrupts alignment in early layers. They show that selectively freezing early layers preserves alignment and improves cross-lingual generalization, supporting their framework and highlighting the importance of interlingual alignment for scalable multilingual learning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies primarily in the introduction of the ILO metric and its application to analyze and improve cross-lingual alignment in LLMs. The conceptual framework distinguishing between core and fragmented interlingual subspaces offers a structured perspective on the topic. While previous work has investigated interlingual representations, the ILO metric provides a more granular and quantifiable measure of alignment consistency across languages, linguistic typology, and resource levels, along with a method to mitigate some of the problems.

**Significance:** The findings have several important implications for the field:

*   **Quantifying Interlingual Alignment:** The ILO metric provides a practical tool for researchers to evaluate and compare the interlingual quality of different LLMs.
*   **Understanding Single-Language Finetuning Effects:** The paper identifies the disruptive effects of single-language fine-tuning on interlingual alignment, providing insights into the potential for catastrophic forgetting in multilingual models.
*   **Improving Cross-Lingual Generalization:**  The selective freezing strategy offers a tangible approach for preserving interlingual alignment during fine-tuning, leading to improved cross-lingual transfer performance.

**Strengths:**

*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The authors employ a combination of qualitative analysis (t-SNE visualizations) and quantitative metrics (ILO) to support their claims.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The study considers a diverse set of languages, linguistic families, and resource levels, increasing the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Practical Solution:**  The selective freezing strategy offers a simple but effective technique for improving cross-lingual performance in LLMs.
*   **Clear Problem Definition**: The paper frames the problem of understanding and quantifying interlingual representations effectively and provides a strong justification for the approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Dataset**: While the mathematical reasoning task is language-agnostic, MGSM could still introduce dataset biases, as it is built upon translated English instructions. The transferability to more general tasks should be analyzed.
*   **Limited Scope of Intervention**: Freezing parameters is a blunt instrument. The study could benefit from exploring more sophisticated methods for guiding fine-tuning while maintaining interlingual alignment.
*   **ILO limitations**: Although the ILO metric offers an interesting method to measure interlingual relationships, it might be limited by the nature of the t-SNE process. If the dataset in t-SNE is not representable and some points overlap incorrectly, the metric might have inaccuracies.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by providing a concrete metric and framework for better understanding interlingual representations in LLMs. Its findings offer both insight into model behavior and a practical approach to improving cross-lingual transfer. While the limitations mentioned above prevent a higher score, the work is clearly above average and has the potential to influence future research in multilingual NLP.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### GNNs as Predictors of Agentic Workflow Performances
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11301v1)
- **Authors**: Yuanshuo Zhang, Yuchen Hou, Bohan Tang, Shuo Chen, Muhan Zhang, Xiaowen Dong, Siheng Chen
- **Abstract**: Agentic workflows invoked by Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success in handling complex tasks. However, optimizing such workflows is costly and inefficient in real-world applications due to extensive invocations of LLMs. To fill this gap, this position paper formulates agentic workflows as computational graphs and advocates Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) as efficient predictors of agentic workflow performances, avoiding repeated LLM invocations for evaluation. To empirically ground this position, we construct FLORA-Bench, a unified platform for benchmarking GNNs for predicting agentic workflow performances. With extensive experiments, we arrive at the following conclusion: GNNs are simple yet effective predictors. This conclusion supports new applications of GNNs and a novel direction towards automating agentic workflow optimization. All codes, models, and data are available at https://github.com/youngsoul0731/Flora-Bench.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "GNNs as Predictors of Agentic Workflow Performances":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes using Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) to predict the performance of agentic workflows, which are complex systems where multiple agents collaborate to solve tasks. The authors argue that directly executing and evaluating these workflows using Large Language Models (LLMs) is computationally expensive. They suggest that GNNs, by representing agentic workflows as graphs and learning to predict their performance, offer a more efficient alternative for optimization. To validate this idea, they introduce FLORA-Bench, a new benchmark dataset containing workflow-task pairs and associated performance data. Through experiments on FLORA-Bench, they demonstrate that GNNs can indeed predict workflow performance effectively, suggesting their potential as predictors in the automation of agentic workflow optimization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using GNNs to predict the performance of agentic workflows is relatively novel. While GNNs have been used in various prediction tasks involving graph-structured data, their application to agentic workflow performance assessment seems to be a new avenue. However, the paper doesn't introduce any novel GNN architectures or training techniques specifically tailored to the agentic workflow domain. It relies on existing GNN models. The primary novelty lies in the application area and the creation of a specialized benchmark.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a relevant and increasingly important problem: the high computational cost of optimizing agentic workflows that rely heavily on LLMs. If GNNs can indeed provide accurate and efficient performance predictions, it would significantly accelerate the development and deployment of such systems. The FLORA-Bench dataset is also a significant contribution, providing a valuable resource for future research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem and motivates the need for efficient performance prediction in agentic workflows.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The methodology for representing agentic workflows as graphs and training GNNs is well-defined.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The introduction of FLORA-Bench and the experimental results on this benchmark provide empirical support for the authors' claims.
    *   **Good Benchmark:** The platform FLORA-Bench consists of large-scale workflow task pairs in several domains is a good start for others.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited GNN Innovation:** The paper relies on existing GNN architectures without proposing any modifications or novel architectures specifically designed for the characteristics of agentic workflow graphs.
    *   **Generalization Concerns:** The limited performance in cross-domain tests raises concerns about the generalization ability of the GNNs. The paper acknowledges this limitation but could provide more insights into why generalization is difficult and how it might be addressed.
    *   **Limited LLM selection:** The authors selected only some LLMs to perform the work. We still need further investigation on the generality on different LLMs.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in agentic workflow optimization. By demonstrating the potential of GNNs and providing the FLORA-Bench dataset, it encourages further exploration of this approach and spurs the development of more sophisticated GNN-based prediction models.

*   **Missing Considerations:** The paper does not address some important practical considerations:

    *   **Data dependency and error propagation**: When the individual agent's performance highly depends on the generated data from previous agents. And the generation may be noisy and uncertain. Therefore, the individual agent's performance may not be representative.
    *   **Explainability**: Understanding *why* a GNN makes certain performance predictions is important for building trust in model and provide insights on improving workflow design.
    *   **Dynamic Workflows**: The current approach uses static workflows and the field may need the ability to model the dynamically adapting workflows as agents respond to the situation and task requirements change.

**Overall Score:**

Given the novelty of applying GNNs to the agentic workflow domain, the significance of the problem addressed, the well-defined methodology, and the contribution of the FLORA-Bench dataset, while also acknowledging the limitations related to model innovation and generalization, I'd give the paper a score of **7**. The paper presents a promising approach with a valuable benchmark, but further research is needed to address the identified weaknesses and fully realize the potential of GNNs in this field.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Are formal and functional linguistic mechanisms dissociated?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11302v1)
- **Authors**: Michael Hanna, Sandro Pezzelle, Yonatan Belinkov
- **Abstract**: Although large language models (LLMs) are increasingly capable, these capabilities are unevenly distributed: they excel at formal linguistic tasks, such as producing fluent, grammatical text, but struggle more with functional linguistic tasks like reasoning and consistent fact retrieval. Inspired by neuroscience, recent work suggests that to succeed on both formal and functional linguistic tasks, LLMs should use different mechanisms for each; such localization could either be built-in or emerge spontaneously through training. In this paper, we ask: do current models, with fast-improving functional linguistic abilities, exhibit distinct localization of formal and functional linguistic mechanisms? We answer this by finding and comparing the "circuits", or minimal computational subgraphs, responsible for various formal and functional tasks. Comparing 5 LLMs across 10 distinct tasks, we find that while there is indeed little overlap between circuits for formal and functional tasks, there is also little overlap between formal linguistic tasks, as exists in the human brain. Thus, a single formal linguistic network, unified and distinct from functional task circuits, remains elusive. However, in terms of cross-task faithfulness - the ability of one circuit to solve another's task - we observe a separation between formal and functional mechanisms, suggesting that shared mechanisms between formal tasks may exist.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether formal and functional linguistic mechanisms are dissociated in large language models (LLMs), drawing inspiration from neuroscience research on the human brain. The authors employ mechanistic interpretability techniques, specifically "circuits," to identify and compare the computational subgraphs responsible for various formal (e.g., syntax, morphology) and functional (e.g., reasoning, world knowledge) linguistic tasks.  They compare circuits across five LLMs and ten tasks, measuring their similarity based on overlap (Intersection over Union, IoU) and cross-task faithfulness (the ability of one circuit to perform another's task). The key findings are that while there's little overlap between circuits for formal and functional tasks, there's also limited overlap *within* formal linguistic tasks, unlike the human brain. However, cross-task faithfulness reveals a separation: formal task circuits perform other formal tasks better than functional ones, and vice versa. The authors conclude that evidence for a unified, distinct formal linguistic network remains elusive, although a formal-functional separation emerges in terms of how well the circuits generalize to other tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of mechanistic interpretability to explore the formal-functional dissociation hypothesis in LLMs *is* relatively novel. Previous work in interpretability has focused on other linguistic phenomena like bias or specific task abilities.  Applying concepts from neuroscience, although increasingly common, is a sensible and potentially impactful direction. Furthermore, conducting this type of analysis across a reasonably sized set of models (5) and tasks (10) sets it apart from much earlier work focused on smaller models or fewer tasks. However, the core techniques (circuit extraction, IoU, ablation-based faithfulness) are well-established, so the novelty primarily lies in the application of these methods to this specific research question.

*   **Significance:** The significance hinges on the paper's ability to inform future LLM development and our understanding of how these models process language. If a clear dissociation were found, it would provide a strong argument for building models with explicit formal-functional modularity. The somewhat ambiguous results—limited circuit overlap but some signal in cross-task faithfulness—suggest the relationship is more nuanced.

    *   **Strengths:**
        *   **Clear Research Question:** The paper addresses a well-defined and relevant question regarding the organization of linguistic knowledge in LLMs.
        *   **Methodological Rigor:** The authors employ established and well-justified methods for circuit extraction and evaluation.
        *   **Relatively Large-Scale Study:** The experiments cover a reasonable range of models and tasks, increasing the generalizability of the findings compared to smaller-scale studies.
        *   **Connection to Neuroscience:**  Linking LLM behavior to brain organization provides a valuable perspective.
    *   **Weaknesses:**
        *   **Ambiguous Results:** The lack of strong, clear-cut evidence for dissociation limits the impact of the findings. The low overall circuit overlap is surprising, and the analysis of the "MLP backbone" doesn't provide a particularly compelling explanation.
        *   **Limited Task Coverage:** While 10 tasks are more than many prior studies, they still only represent a small fraction of the formal and functional linguistic spectrum.
        *   **Interpretability Challenges:** Even with circuits, interpreting *why* certain tasks cluster together or exhibit certain generalization patterns remains difficult and somewhat speculative.
        *   **Metrics:** The reliance on IoU and recall as circuit similarity metrics, although justified, has known limitations, particularly when dealing with circuits of varying sizes. While cross-task faithfulness aims to address this, the link to circuit overlap remains indirect. Furthermore, as noted by the authors, the effectiveness of cross-task faithfulness depends on the IE estimation, which presents a challenge on its own.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper can influence the conversation about how to improve LLMs' reasoning and world knowledge integration. It subtly argues *against* simply assuming that explicit modularity is the immediate solution, suggesting further investigation into the nature of existing implicit separations.  It also provides a roadmap for future research using interpretability to explore cognitive hypotheses in LLMs. However, the inconclusive nature of the findings means its impact will be incremental rather than revolutionary.

**Overall Score Rationale:**

Given the moderate novelty, the methodological rigor, and the potential but limited impact due to ambiguous results, a score of 6 is appropriate. The paper makes a solid contribution to the growing body of work applying interpretability to understand LLMs, but the findings require further investigation to translate into actionable insights for model development. It's more of a "progress report" than a breakthrough discovery.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Unlocking General Long Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Capabilities of Large Language Models via Representation Engineering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11314v1)
- **Authors**: Xinyu Tang, Xiaolei Wang, Zhihao Lv, Yingqian Min, Wayne Xin Zhao, Binbin Hu, Ziqi Liu, Zhiqiang Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in long chain-of-thoughts(long CoTs) have significantly improved the reasoning capabilities of large language models(LLMs). Existing work finds that the capability of long CoT reasoning can be efficiently elicited by tuning on only a few examples and can easily transfer to other tasks. This motivates us to investigate whether long CoT reasoning is a general capability for LLMs. In this work, we conduct an empirical analysis for this question from the perspective of representation. We find that LLMs do encode long CoT reasoning as a general capability, with a clear distinction from vanilla CoTs. Furthermore, domain-specific representations are also required for the effective transfer of long CoT reasoning. Inspired by these findings, we propose GLoRE, a novel representation engineering method to unleash the general long CoT reasoning capabilities of LLMs. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness and efficiency of GLoRE in both in-domain and cross-domain scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs) is a general capability. Through representation engineering, the authors find that LLMs do encode long CoT reasoning as a general capability distinct from vanilla CoTs. However, domain-specific representations are also needed for effective transfer. Based on these findings, they propose GLORE, a novel representation engineering method to unlock the general long CoT reasoning capabilities of LLMs. Experiments demonstrate GLORE's effectiveness and efficiency in both in-domain and cross-domain scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its *analysis of long CoT reasoning from a representation engineering perspective.* Existing work largely focuses on prompting techniques or fine-tuning.  Examining the internal representations to understand and control CoT reasoning is a valuable and under-explored direction. Proposing GLORE based on these findings is a practical contribution. The concept of decoupling long CoT into general reasoning patterns and domain-specific information is interesting.

**Significance:** The findings suggest that LLMs might possess a more general reasoning ability than previously thought, which is significant for future LLM development. The GLORE method offers a training-free approach to enhance reasoning capabilities, which can be practically useful given the cost of fine-tuning. The ability to unlock pre-existing reasoning capabilities within LLMs is impactful as it explores alternative methods without intensive training. The idea of transferring reasoning capabilities across domains also holds promise.

**Strengths:**

*   **Rigorous Analysis:** The paper conducts a detailed empirical analysis, including ablation studies and hyperparameter tuning, to validate its claims.
*   **Clear Presentation:**  The methods and findings are explained clearly, making the paper relatively easy to follow.
*   **Practical Contribution:** GLORE is a training-free method, making it accessible for practical application.
*   **Code Availability:** The code is made available which promotes reproducibility and further exploration of the proposed approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Open-Source LLMs:** The method requires access to the internal representations, thus is infeasible for black-box (closed-source) LLMs, which many of the most powerful LLMs are. This significantly limits the broader applicability.
*   **Limited Scope of Tasks:** While the paper evaluates on several math and science benchmarks, the range of reasoning tasks is still somewhat limited. Evaluating on more diverse and complex reasoning scenarios would strengthen the claims.
*   **Incremental Improvement:** While GLORE outperforms baselines, the improvement, while statistically significant, may not be drastic in all cases. Showing larger performance gains would increase the paper's impact.
*   **Justification of Parameter Selection:** While some parameter analysis is conducted, more justification and a clear methodology on selecting layers for injections should be addressed.

**Justification of Score:**

Overall, the paper offers a novel perspective on understanding long CoT reasoning in LLMs through representation engineering, which I believe is a significant contribution. The analysis is rigourous, and GLORE provides a practical method for enhancing reasoning capabilities. However, the dependence on internal representations of LLMs, limiting its application to open-source models only, and the limited evaluation on more diverse tasks are key limitations. While the approach shows positive results, larger gains would cement the benefits of unlocking long CoT reasoning capabilities.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10
