- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### V-STaR: Benchmarking Video-LLMs on Video Spatio-Temporal Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11495v1)
- **Authors**: Zixu Cheng, Jian Hu, Ziquan Liu, Chenyang Si, Wei Li, Shaogang Gong
- **Abstract**: Human processes video reasoning in a sequential spatio-temporal reasoning logic, we first identify the relevant frames ("when") and then analyse the spatial relationships ("where") between key objects, and finally leverage these relationships to draw inferences ("what"). However, can Video Large Language Models (Video-LLMs) also "reason through a sequential spatio-temporal logic" in videos? Existing Video-LLM benchmarks primarily focus on assessing object presence, neglecting relational reasoning. Consequently, it is difficult to measure whether a model truly comprehends object interactions (actions/events) in videos or merely relies on pre-trained "memory" of co-occurrences as biases in generating answers. In this work, we introduce a Video Spatio-Temporal Reasoning (V-STaR) benchmark to address these shortcomings. The key idea is to decompose video understanding into a Reverse Spatio-Temporal Reasoning (RSTR) task that simultaneously evaluates what objects are present, when events occur, and where they are located while capturing the underlying Chain-of-thought (CoT) logic. To support this evaluation, we construct a dataset to elicit the spatial-temporal reasoning process of Video-LLMs. It contains coarse-to-fine CoT questions generated by a semi-automated GPT-4-powered pipeline, embedding explicit reasoning chains to mimic human cognition. Experiments from 14 Video-LLMs on our V-STaR reveal significant gaps between current Video-LLMs and the needs for robust and consistent spatio-temporal reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "V-STaR: Benchmarking Video-LLMs on Video Spatio-Temporal Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces V-STaR, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the spatio-temporal reasoning capabilities of Video Large Language Models (Video-LLMs). Recognizing that existing benchmarks primarily focus on object identification ("what") without adequately assessing relational reasoning across space and time ("where" and "when"), V-STaR proposes a Reverse Spatio-Temporal Reasoning (RSTR) task. RSTR involves breaking down video understanding into assessing object presence, event occurrence times, and object locations, along with examining how models construct chain-of-thought (CoT) logic during reasoning.  The benchmark includes a dataset with fine-grained, GPT-4 powered CoT questions incorporating "think" tags to mimic human thought processes.  The paper also introduces a new metric, Logarithmic Geometric Mean (LGM), to comprehensively assess performance by combining model scores at each step of the reasoning chain. Experiments on 14 Video-LLMs are conducted, highlighting weaknesses in current models' spatio-temporal reasoning abilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a significant contribution by explicitly targeting spatio-temporal reasoning in Video-LLMs, an area that has been relatively under-explored compared to basic object recognition and question answering. The introduction of the RSTR task and the use of GPT-4 to generate complex, hierarchical CoT questions is a novel approach.  The LGM metric also provides a more nuanced evaluation compared to simple accuracy measures. However, the novelty of individual components (CoT prompts, metric design) is moderate, as these techniques are borrowed from other NLP tasks and applied to the video domain.

*   **Significance:** The V-STaR benchmark addresses a critical gap in the evaluation of Video-LLMs. By exposing the limitations of current models in understanding the relationships between objects, events, and locations in video, the paper motivates future research directions for developing more robust and reliable video understanding systems. The paper's findings could influence the design of future Video-LLM architectures and training procedures.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the problem of inadequate spatio-temporal reasoning evaluation in existing benchmarks.
    *   **Well-Designed Benchmark:** The RSTR task is logically designed and the use of CoT prompts aims to encourage explicit reasoning.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The experiments involve a relatively diverse set of Video-LLMs.
    *   **New Metric:** The LGM metric is a useful addition for evaluating the models at each step of the reasoning chain.
    *   **Reproducibility:** While code availability is necessary, the paper describes the benchmark construction process and evaluation metrics in sufficient detail to allow for future reproduction.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on GPT-4 for Dataset Generation:**  The reliance on GPT-4 to generate the CoT questions introduces potential biases. The quality and diversity of the generated questions are inherently limited by the capabilities and potential biases of GPT-4 itself.  Careful analysis and mitigation of these biases would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Generalization of Findings:** While 14 models were tested, the study could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the architectural factors influencing spatio-temporal reasoning performance. Are there specific model components or training strategies that demonstrably improve performance on V-STaR?
    *   **Scope of Spatio-Temporal Reasoning:** The current V-STaR dataset appears to focus primarily on short-term, relatively explicit spatio-temporal relationships. Expanding the benchmark to include longer-range dependencies, causal relationships, and more complex interactions would be valuable.
    *  **Analysis on failure cases**: The models have made mistakes at which step in CoT questions, and why will make errors in these steps need to deeply analysis.

*   **Potential Influence:** The V-STaR benchmark has the potential to become a standard tool for evaluating spatio-temporal reasoning in Video-LLMs. It provides a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners working in this area. The benchmark could also drive the development of new evaluation metrics and techniques for assessing video understanding models.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by introducing a much-needed benchmark for evaluating spatio-temporal reasoning in Video-LLMs. The RSTR task and the use of CoT prompts are well-designed, and the experiments provide valuable insights into the weaknesses of current models. However, the reliance on GPT-4 for dataset generation introduces potential biases, and the scope of the benchmark could be expanded to include more complex spatio-temporal relationships. Furthermore, the evaluation can go deeper by analyzing the mistake cause in the CoT reasoning chain.  Despite these limitations, the paper addresses a critical gap in the evaluation of Video-LLMs and has the potential to significantly influence future research in this area. Given these factors, a score of 7.5 reflects the paper's substantial, yet improvable contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### HiTVideo: Hierarchical Tokenizers for Enhancing Text-to-Video Generation with Autoregressive Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11513v1)
- **Authors**: Ziqin Zhou, Yifan Yang, Yuqing Yang, Tianyu He, Houwen Peng, Kai Qiu, Qi Dai, Lili Qiu, Chong Luo, Lingqiao Liu
- **Abstract**: Text-to-video generation poses significant challenges due to the inherent complexity of video data, which spans both temporal and spatial dimensions. It introduces additional redundancy, abrupt variations, and a domain gap between language and vision tokens while generation. Addressing these challenges requires an effective video tokenizer that can efficiently encode video data while preserving essential semantic and spatiotemporal information, serving as a critical bridge between text and vision. Inspired by the observation in VQ-VAE-2 and workflows of traditional animation, we propose HiTVideo for text-to-video generation with hierarchical tokenizers. It utilizes a 3D causal VAE with a multi-layer discrete token framework, encoding video content into hierarchically structured codebooks. Higher layers capture semantic information with higher compression, while lower layers focus on fine-grained spatiotemporal details, striking a balance between compression efficiency and reconstruction quality. Our approach efficiently encodes longer video sequences (e.g., 8 seconds, 64 frames), reducing bits per pixel (bpp) by approximately 70\% compared to baseline tokenizers, while maintaining competitive reconstruction quality. We explore the trade-offs between compression and reconstruction, while emphasizing the advantages of high-compressed semantic tokens in text-to-video tasks. HiTVideo aims to address the potential limitations of existing video tokenizers in text-to-video generation tasks, striving for higher compression ratios and simplify LLMs modeling under language guidance, offering a scalable and promising framework for advancing text to video generation. Demo page: https://ziqinzhou66.github.io/project/HiTVideo.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "HiTVideo: Hierarchical Tokenizers for Enhancing Text-to-Video Generation with Autoregressive Large Language Models" introduces HiTVideo, a novel video tokenizer designed for text-to-video generation using large language models (LLMs). The core idea is to use a hierarchical, multi-layer discrete token framework inspired by VQ-VAE-2 and animation workflows. The tokenizer encodes video content into hierarchically structured codebooks, where higher layers capture semantic information with high compression, and lower layers focus on fine-grained spatiotemporal details. The paper claims that HiTVideo improves compression ratios (reducing bits per pixel) while maintaining competitive reconstruction quality and simplifying LLM modeling for text-to-video generation. The paper also explores dynamic encoding and masked decoding techniques to improve efficiency. They train a Llama-3B model to predict the next video token autoregressively, conditioned on text prompts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The use of hierarchical tokenizers for video is not entirely novel (VQ-VAE-2 already did this for images), but the specific adaptation to *video* and the *integration with LLMs for autoregressive generation* is where some of the novelty lies. Prior work in video tokenization has mostly focused on diffusion-based models. The idea of aligning with animation workflows by creating a hierarchy of semantic and detailed tokens is a decent conceptual contribution. The masking and dynamic encoding aspects provide incremental improvements rather than groundbreaking advancements. The combination of these elements is what makes the paper stand apart, though the individual pieces are somewhat derivative.
*   **Significance:**  The significance of the paper lies in its potential to address the computational bottlenecks of text-to-video generation, particularly the high cost of processing video data with LLMs. The hierarchical tokenization can reduce the token counts significantly, making it feasible to generate longer videos with less memory. While diffusion models are currently popular for text-to-video, the paper argues that LLMs offer advantages in terms of speed and multi-modality integration if they can be made efficient. The hierarchical encoding simplifies training and improves semantic alignment which can have a noticeable impact. The results support the claim of improved compression and reconstruction quality. Also, they've focused primarily on proving a concept instead of optimizing an autoregressive LLM.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses an important problem: Efficient video tokenization for LLM-based generation.
    *   The hierarchical approach is well-motivated and grounded in existing work.
    *   Demonstrated superior compression with competitive reconstruction quality.
    *   Provides qualitative evidence of improved coherence in text-to-video generation.
    *   Dynamic encoding and masking offer improvements in efficiency and adaptability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The overall method is incremental; individual components are not groundbreaking.
    *   Experiments primarily focus on reconstruction quality; more comprehensive text-to-video generation evaluations (e.g., user studies) are needed to demonstrate the full impact. The quantitative results section is dominated by the reconstruction measures instead of the generative ones.
    *   Comparisons could be more exhaustive (especially against recent transformer-based video models).
    *   The reliance on Llama-3B is okay to show proof of concept but limits its direct comparison to state of the art diffusion models. It isn't clear that they are using an optimized implementation.
    *   The contribution of each individual component (e.g. the masked decoding or type of loss function) could be quantified more thoroughly.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the method proves to be scalable and robust, it could influence the design of future text-to-video generation systems. It can spur further research on hierarchical representations and their role in bridging the gap between language and visual tokens. The demonstration that such a technique can significantly compress video while remaining compatible with an LLM architecture makes this work somewhat influential.

**Justification for Score:**

A score of 6 is warranted. The paper provides a solid, well-executed implementation of hierarchical tokenization for videos. While not revolutionary, it presents a compelling approach for making LLM-based text-to-video generation more efficient. The main weaknesses lie in the incremental nature of the work and the limited evaluation on full-fledged text-to-video generation tasks compared to current SoTA methods. A higher score would require significantly more novel contributions and thorough evaluation against other strong baselines.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Potential of large language model-powered nudges for promoting daily water and energy conservation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11531v1)
- **Authors**: Zonghan Li, Song Tong, Yi Liu, Kaiping Peng, Chunyan Wang
- **Abstract**: The increasing amount of pressure related to water and energy shortages has increased the urgency of cultivating individual conservation behaviors. While the concept of nudging, i.e., providing usage-based feedback, has shown promise in encouraging conservation behaviors, its efficacy is often constrained by the lack of targeted and actionable content. This study investigates the impact of the use of large language models (LLMs) to provide tailored conservation suggestions for conservation intentions and their rationale. Through a survey experiment with 1,515 university participants, we compare three virtual nudging scenarios: no nudging, traditional nudging with usage statistics, and LLM-powered nudging with usage statistics and personalized conservation suggestions. The results of statistical analyses and causal forest modeling reveal that nudging led to an increase in conservation intentions among 86.9%-98.0% of the participants. LLM-powered nudging achieved a maximum increase of 18.0% in conservation intentions, surpassing traditional nudging by 88.6%. Furthermore, structural equation modeling results reveal that exposure to LLM-powered nudges enhances self-efficacy and outcome expectations while diminishing dependence on social norms, thereby increasing intrinsic motivation to conserve. These findings highlight the transformative potential of LLMs in promoting individual water and energy conservation, representing a new frontier in the design of sustainable behavioral interventions and resource management.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the potential of Large Language Models (LLMs) to enhance the effectiveness of nudges in promoting daily water and energy conservation. It compares three scenarios in a university setting: no nudging, traditional nudging using usage statistics, and LLM-powered nudging with personalized conservation suggestions. The study finds that LLM-powered nudges significantly increase conservation intentions compared to traditional nudges. Furthermore, the research suggests that LLM-powered nudges operate by strengthening self-efficacy and outcome expectations, while diminishing reliance on social norms, leading to increased intrinsic motivation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of LLMs with behavioral nudges is relatively novel in the context of resource conservation. While both nudging and LLMs have been studied extensively, their integration to personalize conservation tips based on usage statistics is an interesting innovation. However, using LLMs to generate personalized content is becoming more common, which slightly diminishes the absolute novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in demonstrating the potential of LLMs to personalize behavioral interventions and improve their effectiveness. This approach offers a cost-effective way to encourage sustainable behaviors compared to traditional, more resource-intensive methods. The finding that LLM-powered nudges boost intrinsic motivation, as opposed to just short-term behavioral changes prompted by social norms, is particularly valuable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The study utilizes a randomized controlled trial with a relatively large sample size, increasing the reliability of the findings.
    *   **Statistical Analysis:** The paper employs a diverse array of statistical techniques, including multi-arm causal forests and structural equation modeling, to provide a comprehensive understanding of the intervention's impact and underlying mechanisms.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The findings are directly applicable to resource management, especially in university campuses and potentially broader urban settings.
    *   **Addresses a Timely Issue:** Water and energy conservation are critical challenges, making this study's contribution relevant and timely.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Intention vs. Behavior:** The study primarily measures conservation intentions, which are not always perfectly correlated with actual behavior. Future research should validate these findings by observing real-world changes in water and energy consumption.
    *   **Limited Context:** The study is conducted within a specific university environment, limiting the generalizability to other populations or settings. Cultural factors could play a significant role.
    *   **LLM Content Evaluation:** While participants rated usage statistics as most important, they ranked LLM suggestions lower. This suggests potential issues with the quality or framing of the suggestions that should be investigated further.
    *   **Potential for the Black Box Effect:** The study acknowledges that participants may not consciously perceive the LLM-generated content. More explanation is needed on how this black-box effect relates to the goal of intrinsic motivation, where greater awareness and acceptance may be desired.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the design of future behavioral interventions in resource management and other domains. It highlights the potential of AI to personalize and optimize nudges, leading to more effective and sustainable behavioral changes.
    *   **LLM Specificity:** The paper does not explicitly specify which LLM was used. Clarity about the model can improve reproducibility.

*   **Overall:** While the paper has some limitations, its methodological rigor, practical implications, and focus on a timely issue justify a relatively high rating. It offers valuable insights into the application of LLMs to behavioral interventions.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper offers a valuable contribution to the field by exploring a novel approach to resource conservation using LLMs. The solid methodology and comprehensive statistical analysis strengthen the findings. However, the focus on intentions rather than actual behaviors, limitations in generalizability, and questions regarding LLM content evaluation prevent a higher score. The paper will serve as a starting point for additional research into how AI can be used to promote conservation and other pro-social behaviors.

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### Similarity-Aware Token Pruning: Your VLM but Faster
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11549v1)
- **Authors**: Ahmadreza Jeddi, Negin Baghbanzadeh, Elham Dolatabadi, Babak Taati
- **Abstract**: The computational demands of Vision Transformers (ViTs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs) remain a significant challenge due to the quadratic complexity of self-attention. While token pruning offers a promising solution, existing methods often introduce training overhead or fail to adapt dynamically across layers. We present SAINT, a training-free token pruning framework that leverages token similarity and a graph-based formulation to dynamically optimize pruning rates and redundancy thresholds. Through systematic analysis, we identify a universal three-stage token evolution process (aligner-explorer-aggregator) in transformers, enabling aggressive pruning in early stages without sacrificing critical information. For ViTs, SAINT doubles the throughput of ViT-H/14 at 224px with only 0.6% accuracy loss on ImageNet-1K, surpassing the closest competitor by 0.8%. For VLMs, we apply SAINT in three modes: ViT-only, LLM-only, and hybrid. SAINT reduces LLaVA-13B's tokens by 75%, achieving latency comparable to LLaVA-7B with less than 1% performance loss across benchmarks. Our work establishes a unified, practical framework for efficient inference in ViTs and VLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents "SAINT" (Similarity-Aware INference Time Token Pruning), a training-free framework to reduce the computational demands of Vision Transformers (ViTs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs.  SAINT leverages token similarity and a graph-based formulation to dynamically optimize pruning rates and redundancy thresholds. The key idea is that transformers exhibit a three-stage token evolution process (aligner-explorer-aggregator), and early layers (the "aligner" stage) can be pruned aggressively without significantly impacting performance. The method is applied to both ViTs and VLMs, demonstrating improved throughput with minimal accuracy loss.  For ViTs, it significantly improves throughput on ImageNet. For VLMs, it's applied in ViT-only, LLM-only, and hybrid modes, reducing latency to levels comparable to smaller models with minimal performance degradation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a unified approach to token pruning that considers token dynamics and leverages a graph-based similarity measure. Identifying the aligner-explorer-aggregator stages in transformer layers and exploiting this knowledge for efficient token pruning is a key innovative point. While similarity-based token pruning exists (ToMe, ToFu), SAINT's dynamic pruning rate adjustment and graph-based ranking, particularly emphasizing early layer pruning, sets it apart. The application across both ViTs and VLMs is a strength. However, the individual components (similarity measure, graph representation) are not fundamentally novel; the novelty lies in their combination and the dynamic adjustment.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it directly addresses the computational bottleneck of ViTs and VLMs. A training-free inference-time method is highly desirable as it avoids the overhead of retraining or fine-tuning.  The demonstrated performance improvements – doubling throughput in ViTs with negligible accuracy loss, and significant latency reduction in VLMs – are practically impactful.  By making larger VLMs more efficient, this work potentially broadens their applicability.  Also, It demonstrates that optimizing *purely* for FLOPs might not align with real-world efficiency, and practical implementation matters.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Unified Framework:** The method provides a single framework applicable to both ViTs and VLMs, suggesting generalizability.
    *   **Training-Free:** Avoids the expensive training/fine-tuning overhead.
    *   **Performance Gains:** Demonstrates impressive speedups with minimal accuracy loss.
    *   **Systematic Analysis:** The investigation of token dynamics across transformer layers is thorough and provides a good foundation.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments cover a range of architectures, benchmarks, and pruning strategies.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvements:** The method builds upon existing similarity-based pruning techniques (ToMe) and does not introduce fundamentally new concepts in similarity calculation or graph representation.
    *   **Parameter Sensitivity:** Although the authors claim robust results with certain parameters, the necessity of tuning 'K', 'gamma', and 'tau' per architecture might pose a challenge in deployment. More work on automated parameter tuning would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited FLOPs Reduction:** The analysis shows that the GFLOPs reduction is not as significant as that achieved by other methods. This is a critical point, as the study positions itself as one that emphasizes the practical benefits beyond theoretical FLOPs. The authors should have made a more thorough comparison of practical considerations vs. the degree of FLOPs reduction.

*   **Potential Impact:** The work can influence the field by:
    *   Guiding future research towards dynamic, similarity-aware pruning strategies.
    *   Encouraging a focus on practical inference efficiency (latency, throughput) rather than just theoretical FLOPs.
    *   Providing a readily deployable technique for accelerating ViTs and VLMs.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

While SAINT demonstrates practical performance gains and offers a valuable training-free solution for accelerating ViTs and VLMs, the lack of significant theoretical advancements, particularly the incremental improvements on existing methods. The practical benefits are high and can significantly impact real-world application, its weaknesses cannot be ignored. I give it a moderate novelty score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### VERIFY: A Benchmark of Visual Explanation and Reasoning for Investigating Multimodal Reasoning Fidelity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11557v1)
- **Authors**: Jing Bi, Junjia Guo, Susan Liang, Guangyu Sun, Luchuan Song, Yunlong Tang, Jinxi He, Jiarui Wu, Ali Vosoughi, Chen Chen, Chenliang Xu
- **Abstract**: Visual reasoning is central to human cognition, enabling individuals to interpret and abstractly understand their environment. Although recent Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated impressive performance across language and vision-language tasks, existing benchmarks primarily measure recognition-based skills and inadequately assess true visual reasoning capabilities. To bridge this critical gap, we introduce VERIFY, a benchmark explicitly designed to isolate and rigorously evaluate the visual reasoning capabilities of state-of-the-art MLLMs. VERIFY compels models to reason primarily from visual information, providing minimal textual context to reduce reliance on domain-specific knowledge and linguistic biases. Each problem is accompanied by a human-annotated reasoning path, making it the first to provide in-depth evaluation of model decision-making processes. Additionally, we propose novel metrics that assess visual reasoning fidelity beyond mere accuracy, highlighting critical imbalances in current model reasoning patterns. Our comprehensive benchmarking of leading MLLMs uncovers significant limitations, underscoring the need for a balanced and holistic approach to both perception and reasoning. For more teaser and testing, visit our project page (https://verify-eqh.pages.dev/).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "VERIFY: A Benchmark of Visual Explanation and Reasoning for Investigating Multimodal Reasoning Fidelity":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VERIFY, a new benchmark designed to rigorously evaluate the visual reasoning capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). The benchmark focuses on isolating visual reasoning by minimizing reliance on textual context, compelling models to primarily reason from visual information.  Each problem is accompanied by a human-annotated reasoning path, enabling in-depth evaluation of the model's decision-making process. The paper also proposes novel metrics beyond accuracy to assess visual reasoning fidelity, highlighting imbalances in current model reasoning patterns. Through benchmarking leading MLLMs, the authors uncover limitations, underscoring the need for a balanced approach to perception and reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by introducing a benchmark specifically tailored to assess visual reasoning fidelity, rather than relying on tasks primarily measuring recognition. The use of minimal textual context and focus on visual information as the primary driver for reasoning is a key strength. The human-annotated reasoning paths are also a significant addition, offering a deeper level of insight than simple accuracy metrics. However, the source data from the China's National Civil Servants Examination isn't universally accessible. While it allows for real-world scenarios rather than synthesized, it limits reproducibility and comparability in some settings. Using different prompts for different models can affect results in non-predictable manners and affect the credibility of the overall evaluation.
*   **Significance:** The benchmark addresses a critical gap in the evaluation of MLLMs, which often conflate visual reasoning with domain knowledge embedded in the LLM component. VERIFY's design promotes a more accurate assessment of visual reasoning capabilities in isolation. The identified limitations in existing MLLMs' reasoning patterns are significant and highlight the need for improved models that balance perception and abstract reasoning. The qualitative analyses of model behaviors are valuable, providing a deeper understanding of where models struggle (e.g., semantic dominance bias). However, the experimental evaluation section focuses primarily on accuracy and high-level analysis. Additional investigation into the correlation between visual features, model architectures, and reasoning fidelity will improve the credibility.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Focused Benchmark:** Addresses a specific and crucial aspect of MLLM evaluation (visual reasoning fidelity).
    *   **Human-Annotated Reasoning Paths:** Provides a unique and valuable resource for in-depth analysis.
    *   **Novel Metrics:** Moves beyond simple accuracy to assess the reasoning process itself.
    *   **Detailed Qualitative Analysis:** Offers insights into model behaviors and limitations.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Data Source:** Relying on a single real-world dataset limits generalizability and accessibility.
    *   **Limited Model Diversity:** The model comparison lacks of visual features analysis to justify its argument.
    *   **Evaluation Depth:** The experimental section focuses primarily on accuracy and high-level analysis.
    *   **Overconfidence of LLM Judge:** The work doesn't provide more information regarding the LLM used as judge, for example, hyperparameters setting, prompt or more analyses about it.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Hard to reproduce without the annotation procedure and analysis.

*   **Potential Influence:** VERIFY has the potential to become a standard benchmark for evaluating the visual reasoning capabilities of MLLMs, influencing the development of more robust and balanced models. The detailed reasoning paths and qualitative analysis can inform future research directions in multimodal AI.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of MLLM evaluation by addressing a critical gap and providing a novel benchmark with detailed reasoning paths. However, the reliance on a specific dataset, the limited evaluation depth, the lack of a solid theoretic backbone and analysis related to visual data processing weaken the findings and the generalizability of the conclusions. It's valuable, but not transformative.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Implicit Bias-Like Patterns in Reasoning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11572v1)
- **Authors**: Messi H. J. Lee, Calvin K. Lai
- **Abstract**: Implicit bias refers to automatic or spontaneous mental processes that shape perceptions, judgments, and behaviors. Previous research examining `implicit bias' in large language models (LLMs) has often approached the phenomenon differently than how it is studied in humans by focusing primarily on model outputs rather than on model processing. To examine model processing, we present a method called the Reasoning Model Implicit Association Test (RM-IAT) for studying implicit bias-like patterns in reasoning models: LLMs that employ step-by-step reasoning to solve complex tasks. Using this method, we find that reasoning models require more tokens when processing association-incompatible information compared to association-compatible information. These findings suggest AI systems harbor patterns in processing information that are analogous to human implicit bias. We consider the implications of these implicit bias-like patterns for their deployment in real-world applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and rigorous evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Reasoning Model Implicit Association Test (RM-IAT), a novel method to investigate implicit bias-like patterns in reasoning models. Unlike previous research that primarily focuses on model outputs, the RM-IAT analyzes the model's processing effort, measured by the number of reasoning tokens used, when dealing with association-compatible and association-incompatible information. The authors find that reasoning models, specifically OpenAI's `03-mini`, tend to expend more computational effort (as indicated by higher token counts) when processing information that contradicts established associations, similar to how humans exhibit slower response times in IATs when processing incompatible pairings. They found this pattern across nine out of ten RM-IATs. The paper also notes the occurrences of refusals (model non-compliance), particularly in RM-IATs related to racial associations, and discusses potential implications for the deployment of AI systems in real-world decision-making contexts. The paper also addresses limitations, such as the measurement error introduced by the granularity of reasoning token counts, and suggests directions for future research.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's strength lies primarily in its methodological innovation. Shifting the focus from model *outputs* (which can be superficially aligned through post-training) to model *processing* offers a deeper, more nuanced understanding of potential biases within AI systems.  The RM-IAT provides a framework akin to psychophysical measures in cognitive psychology. This is a crucial step, as it acknowledges that even models seemingly devoid of explicit bias can still exhibit patterns in their internal operations that reflect underlying biases prevalent in their training data or architecture.  The idea of using reasoning tokens as a proxy for cognitive effort is also clever and well-argued, drawing a direct parallel to response latency in human IATs.

However, several points temper the paper's overall impact:

*   **Limited Scope:** The study predominantly relies on OpenAI's `03-mini`.  While the choice is practical, it's a single point of failure for generalizability.  The claim that the observations represent inherent traits of *reasoning models* as a class requires broader empirical validation across different architectures and training paradigms. The mention of DeepSeek R1 does not translate into usage.
*   **Interpretational Caveats:** While the authors draw a strong analogy to human implicit bias, it's important to acknowledge that the underlying mechanisms are fundamentally different. Equating reasoning tokens with cognitive effort is an interpretation, and further exploration of the content of these tokens would strengthen this connection.  It’s possible the increased token usage reflects something else entirely, like a more circuitous or convoluted path to a similar answer, not necessarily a biased one.
*   **Effect Size and Refusals:** The effect sizes are medium to large, but require closer scrutiny given the model refusal rate on prompts with racial connotations. While the authors offer explanations related to RLHF, this highlights a critical question: are these patterns genuine indications of bias, or artifacts of the model's attempt to adhere to safety constraints?
*   **Lack of Real-World Consequence Exploration:** While the paper acknowledges the potential for real-world impacts, it doesn't provide concrete examples or scenarios where these implicit bias-like patterns would lead to demonstrably unfair or discriminatory outcomes. This lack of practical illustration diminishes the paper's immediate relevance. There is also no consideration that RLHF is intended to avoid causing discriminatory outcomes.
*   **Absence of Countermeasures:** The paper identifies a concerning trend but offers no specific methods for mitigating or addressing these implicit-like biases. This limits its utility for AI practitioners working to develop fairer systems.

The novelty, therefore, lies in the method, but the significance hinges on future work that addresses these limitations.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and important methodology for probing the *internal processing* of language models.  However, this is balanced against several crucial factors. The reliance on a single model limits the generalizability. The findings are correlational rather than causal. The refusal patterns complicates interpretation. The implications are largely theoretical. Therefore, this paper has potential to influence future research, but its current impact is limited by the points addressed above.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 6/10

### Synthesizing Access Control Policies using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11573v1)
- **Authors**: Adarsh Vatsa, Pratyush Patel, William Eiers
- **Abstract**: Cloud compute systems allow administrators to write access control policies that govern access to private data. While policies are written in convenient languages, such as AWS Identity and Access Management Policy Language, manually written policies often become complex and error prone. In this paper, we investigate whether and how well Large Language Models (LLMs) can be used to synthesize access control policies. Our investigation focuses on the task of taking an access control request specification and zero-shot prompting LLMs to synthesize a well-formed access control policy which correctly adheres to the request specification. We consider two scenarios, one which the request specification is given as a concrete list of requests to be allowed or denied, and another in which a natural language description is used to specify sets of requests to be allowed or denied. We then argue that for zero-shot prompting, more precise and structured prompts using a syntax based approach are necessary and experimentally show preliminary results validating our approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for synthesizing access control policies, specifically focusing on AWS IAM policies. The authors investigate how LLMs perform when prompted with different types of request specifications: concrete lists of requests, natural language descriptions, and a more structured syntax. They evaluate the generated policies by comparing them to ground-truth policies using SMT solving and permissiveness analysis. The results indicate that LLMs can generate syntactically valid policies, but their accuracy and permissiveness vary significantly depending on the prompt's structure. The structured syntax approach yielded better results than natural language prompts, suggesting that LLMs benefit from more precise guidance in this task.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of LLMs to access control policy synthesis is relatively novel, although prior works have explored LLMs as coding assistants for security tasks. The study of different prompting strategies (concrete requests, coarse-grained natural language, fine-grained with syntax) and their impact on policy synthesis is a worthwhile contribution. The evaluation methodology, combining SMT solving and permissiveness analysis, builds upon existing techniques but applies them specifically to the context of LLM-generated policies. However, using LLMs for code generation, in general, is now a widely explored field, so applying it to access control policies, while useful, is not a fundamental breakthrough.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to automate the creation of access control policies, a task that is often complex and error-prone. If LLMs can reliably generate accurate and secure policies, it could significantly reduce the burden on cloud administrators and improve overall security posture. The findings regarding the importance of structured prompts provide valuable insights for designing effective interfaces for policy synthesis tools. The initial success of the syntax-based prompting suggests a potential path toward creating a more usable domain-specific language to enhance LLM accuracy.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and research questions.
    *   Well-defined methodology with three distinct prompting approaches.
    *   Rigorous evaluation using SMT solving and permissiveness analysis.
    *   Insightful findings regarding the impact of prompt structure.
    *   Identifies promising future research directions, such as developing dedicated policy specification languages.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited dataset of policies (47 policies), although it has been enhanced from previously published datasets. This limits the generalizability of the results.
    *   Zero-shot prompting may not be the most effective approach. Fine-tuning LLMs on access control policy data could potentially yield better results.
    *   The evaluation primarily focuses on permissiveness, while other aspects of policy quality, such as redundancy and complexity, are not explicitly addressed.
    *   The "fine-grained with syntax" prompt, while better than natural language, still requires a software engineer to craft, limiting its practicality for general users.
    *   The reliance on GPT-4 limits reproducibility and makes comparisons across different LLMs difficult.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence future research in several ways:

    *   Encourage the development of dedicated policy specification languages tailored for LLM-based synthesis.
    *   Stimulate research on fine-tuning LLMs for access control policy tasks.
    *   Promote the development of more comprehensive evaluation metrics for assessing the quality of LLM-generated policies.
    *   Inspire the creation of user-friendly interfaces that guide users in crafting structured prompts for policy synthesis.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid initial investigation into the use of LLMs for access control policy synthesis. The novelty lies in the application of LLMs to this specific domain and the comparison of different prompting strategies. While the results are not groundbreaking, they provide valuable insights into the challenges and opportunities associated with this approach. The paper is well-written and the methodology is sound. However, the limited dataset and the reliance on zero-shot prompting somewhat constrain the impact and generalizability of the findings. Given these considerations, the paper warrants a score of 6. The work shows potential, but requires further work to mature it into a deployable solution.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Vamba: Understanding Hour-Long Videos with Hybrid Mamba-Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11579v1)
- **Authors**: Weiming Ren, Wentao Ma, Huan Yang, Cong Wei, Ge Zhang, Wenhu Chen
- **Abstract**: State-of-the-art transformer-based large multimodal models (LMMs) struggle to handle hour-long video inputs due to the quadratic complexity of the causal self-attention operations, leading to high computational costs during training and inference. Existing token compression-based methods reduce the number of video tokens but often incur information loss and remain inefficient for extremely long sequences. In this paper, we explore an orthogonal direction to build a hybrid Mamba-Transformer model (VAMBA) that employs Mamba-2 blocks to encode video tokens with linear complexity. Without any token reduction, VAMBA can encode more than 1024 frames (640$\times$360) on a single GPU, while transformer-based models can only encode 256 frames. On long video input, VAMBA achieves at least 50% reduction in GPU memory usage during training and inference, and nearly doubles the speed per training step compared to transformer-based LMMs. Our experimental results demonstrate that VAMBA improves accuracy by 4.3% on the challenging hour-long video understanding benchmark LVBench over prior efficient video LMMs, and maintains strong performance on a broad spectrum of long and short video understanding tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VAMBA, a hybrid Mamba-Transformer model designed to enhance the handling of long-duration video inputs in large multimodal models (LMMs).  It addresses the quadratic complexity limitations of self-attention in transformers, which makes processing hour-long videos computationally expensive. VAMBA employs Mamba-2 blocks for efficient video token encoding with linear complexity and utilizes cross-attention to update text tokens based on video content. The authors claim VAMBA achieves significant memory reduction, faster training, and improved accuracy on long video understanding benchmarks like LVBench compared to other efficient video LMMs. The paper includes ablation studies and comparisons with existing models to demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed architecture.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of using a hybrid Mamba-Transformer architecture to address the long-context limitations in video LMMs is reasonably novel, although the individual components (Mamba and Transformers) are well-established. The specific combination and application to the challenge of hour-long video understanding distinguishes it from previous work. Replacing the quadratic self-attention with Mamba-2 blocks for video and cross-attention for text seems like a reasonable approach.
* **Significance:**  The significance lies in the potential to enable LMMs to process extremely long videos that were previously impractical due to computational constraints. This could unlock new applications in video analysis, summarization, and understanding.  The empirical results on the LVBench benchmark show improvements over previous efficient video LMMs, which adds to the credibility.  The gains in memory efficiency and training speed are also practically important for researchers and practitioners.  The claim of maintaining performance across short and long videos is also significant, suggesting VAMBA doesn't compromise on shorter video tasks.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenge of processing long videos with transformer-based LMMs.
    * **Well-Defined Approach:** VAMBA is described in detail, and the rationale for the hybrid architecture is well-explained.
    * **Comprehensive Experiments:** The ablation studies are important for justifying the design choices.  The comparisons with existing models provide context and demonstrate the advantages of VAMBA.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents empirical evidence of improved accuracy and efficiency on relevant benchmarks.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Baseline Comparison:** Although comparisons are made to other "efficient" LMMs, it's less clear how VAMBA compares to state-of-the-art *but not necessarily efficient* LMMs on overall performance, when computational cost is not a limiting factor. A trade-off between efficiency and performance might exist, which should be acknowledged.
    * **Potential Hardware Optimization Issues:** The paper mentions hardware under-optimization for Mamba as a caveat. This suggests that the theoretical performance gains might not be fully realized in practice.  More details on the specific hardware limitations would be useful.
    * **Incremental Improvement:** While the paper demonstrates a 4.3% improvement on LVBench, the overall improvement is incremental.
    * **Lack of Theoretical Analysis:** While the authors provide complexity analysis, a deeper theoretical justification for why this architecture is effective would strengthen the paper.
    * **Limited Code Availability (Assumed):** It is stated code will be made available and this may hinder the rapid adaption of this method until the code can be thoroughly reviewed.
* **Potential Influence:** VAMBA could inspire further research on hybrid architectures that combine the strengths of transformers and state-space models for long-context tasks. It could also influence the development of more efficient video LMMs.
    * **Further evaluation** To strengthen the work, the authors could also explore how well these models transfer to other tasks and investigate the ability to handle videos across more types of vision-and-language tasks.
    * **Further work** The authors should further explore combining this approach with token reduction methods to further optimize performance.
Overall, the paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of video LMMs. The hybrid Mamba-Transformer architecture offers a promising approach for handling long videos more efficiently. The empirical results are encouraging, although the improvements are incremental.  The limitations, particularly regarding hardware optimization and baseline comparison, prevent a higher score.

Score: 7
Rationale: VAMBA offers a reasonably novel and practically relevant solution to an important challenge. The experimental results support the claims. However, certain limitations such as the incremental nature of the improvements, potential hardware bottlenecks, and incremental improvements temper enthusiasm.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Broaden your SCOPE! Efficient Multi-turn Conversation Planning for LLMs using Semantic Space
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11586v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiliang Chen, Xinyuan Niu, Chuan-Sheng Foo, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are used in chatbots or AI assistants to hold conversations with a human user. In such applications, the quality (e.g., user engagement, safety) of a conversation is important and can only be exactly known at the end of the conversation. To maximize its expected quality, conversation planning reasons about the stochastic transitions within a conversation to select the optimal LLM response at each turn. Existing simulation-based conversation planning algorithms typically select the optimal response by simulating future conversations with a large number of LLM queries at every turn. However, this process is extremely time-consuming and hence impractical for real-time conversations. This paper presents a novel approach called Semantic space COnversation Planning with improved Efficiency (SCOPE) that exploits the dense semantic representation of conversations to perform conversation planning efficiently. In particular, SCOPE models the stochastic transitions in conversation semantics and their associated rewards to plan entirely within the semantic space. This allows us to select the optimal LLM response at every conversation turn without needing additional LLM queries for simulation. As a result, SCOPE can perform conversation planning 70 times faster than conventional simulation-based planning algorithms when applied to a wide variety of conversation starters and two reward functions seen in the real world, yet achieving a higher reward within a practical planning budget. Our code can be found at: https://github.com/chenzhiliang94/convo-plan-SCOPE.
- **Summary**: The paper "Broaden Your SCOPE! Efficient Multi-Turn Conversation Planning for LLMs Using Semantic Space" introduces SCOPE, a novel approach for efficient conversation planning in large language models (LLMs). Unlike existing simulation-based methods that rely on costly LLM queries at each turn, SCOPE leverages the dense semantic representation of conversations to perform planning within a semantic space. During an offline learning phase, SCOPE learns lightweight transition and reward models within this semantic space. At runtime, it projects the conversation and candidate responses into the semantic space and uses the learned models to perform Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) without needing additional LLM queries for simulation. The authors demonstrate that SCOPE can plan up to 70 times faster than conventional simulation-based methods while achieving higher cumulative rewards under realistic planning budgets. The approach is evaluated on several conversational settings using two different reward functions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty of this work lies in its ability to drastically reduce the computational cost of conversation planning by operating in a pre-learned semantic space, rather than relying on computationally intensive LLM queries for lookahead simulation. This is a significant departure from standard simulation-based approaches and offers a compelling alternative for real-time conversation planning. The idea of learning a lightweight transition model in semantic space is also relatively novel in the specific context of conversation planning, although similar ideas have been explored in other planning tasks. The use of MCTS within a pre-learned semantic space is not completely new, but the paper demonstrates its effectiveness in a conversation setting with realistic constraints.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is potentially high. The bottleneck of conversation planning has always been the computational burden associated with LLM-based simulation. SCOPE addresses this issue directly, enabling more complex and effective planning strategies to be deployed in real-time applications. The ability to plan efficiently opens the door to improvements in conversation quality (user engagement, safety, etc.) and enables new applications for LLMs in interactive settings. Furthermore, this reduces the cost to LLM providers because it reduces the number of API calls, which can also be a significant constraint. The experiments provide strong evidence that SCOPE outperforms existing methods under practical planning constraints, suggesting that this approach could have a tangible impact on the field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The core idea of operating in a semantic space to reduce computation is well-motivated and clearly explained.
    *   The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating significant speedups and improved performance compared to baseline methods.
    *   The paper provides detailed information about the implementation and evaluation setup, which enhances reproducibility.
    *   The ablation studies offer valuable insights into the influence of different components within SCOPE.
    *   Well written, easy to follow

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The assumption of perfect semantic representations and the faithfulness of transition and reward models learned in this space is questionable, and it would be valuable to explore more robust or adaptive techniques to handle these approximations. While the theory mentions that the MDP equations still result in the same solution with some assumptions around the quality of f, R, and T, the assumptions do not always hold true.
    *   The paper primarily focuses on two specific reward functions. While practical, a more comprehensive evaluation across a wider range of reward functions would strengthen the generality of the findings.
    *   The reliance on a pre-trained semantic embedding model might introduce bias or limit the adaptability of SCOPE to different domains or conversational styles. It would be beneficial to investigate the impact of different embedding models on the performance of SCOPE.
    *   The paper would benefit from an analysis of the limitations of SCOPE. What are the scenarios where SCOPE might fail or underperform compared to other methods?
    *   The implementation uses a Vicuna model, which some might argue is less representative of modern LLMs. Modern LLMs often have significant guardrails that change their behavior. As well, there is mention of GPT-4, but there could be experiments conducted to better compare the different models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in conversation planning, particularly by shifting the focus toward more efficient planning strategies that leverage semantic representations. The SCOPE framework could serve as a foundation for future work exploring adaptive planning techniques, hybrid approaches combining semantic space planning with LLM-based simulation, or methods for learning more robust semantic representations for conversation.

Score: 7

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and efficient approach to conversation planning that has the potential to address a key bottleneck in the field. The experimental results are convincing, and the technical details are well-documented. While the paper does have some limitations, particularly in terms of the breadth of evaluation and the assumptions made about semantic representations, the significance of the core idea and the potential for future research warrant a relatively high score. This is a solid contribution, but it leaves room for further work to address its limitations and explore its full potential.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Pathology Image Compression with Pre-trained Autoencoders
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11591v1)
- **Authors**: Srikar Yellapragada, Alexandros Graikos, Kostas Triaridis, Zilinghan Li, Tarak Nath Nandi, Ravi K Madduri, Prateek Prasanna, Joel Saltz, Dimitris Samaras
- **Abstract**: The growing volume of high-resolution Whole Slide Images in digital histopathology poses significant storage, transmission, and computational efficiency challenges. Standard compression methods, such as JPEG, reduce file sizes but often fail to preserve fine-grained phenotypic details critical for downstream tasks. In this work, we repurpose autoencoders (AEs) designed for Latent Diffusion Models as an efficient learned compression framework for pathology images. We systematically benchmark three AE models with varying compression levels and evaluate their reconstruction ability using pathology foundation models. We introduce a fine-tuning strategy to further enhance reconstruction fidelity that optimizes a pathology-specific learned perceptual metric. We validate our approach on downstream tasks, including segmentation, patch classification, and multiple instance learning, showing that replacing images with AE-compressed reconstructions leads to minimal performance degradation. Additionally, we propose a K-means clustering-based quantization method for AE latents, improving storage efficiency while maintaining reconstruction quality. We provide the weights of the fine-tuned autoencoders at https://huggingface.co/collections/StonyBrook-CVLab/pathology-fine-tuned-aes-67d45f223a659ff2e3402dd0.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel approach to pathology image compression using pre-trained autoencoders (AEs) from Latent Diffusion Models (LDMs). It addresses the limitations of standard compression techniques like JPEG, which often fail to preserve fine-grained phenotypic details crucial for downstream tasks in histopathology.  The authors systematically benchmark three AEs (SD-1.5, SD-3, and DC-AE), evaluating their reconstruction ability using pathology foundation models. They introduce a fine-tuning strategy optimizing a pathology-specific learned perceptual metric to enhance reconstruction fidelity. Finally, they propose a K-means clustering-based quantization method for AE latents, improving storage efficiency while maintaining reconstruction quality. The approach is validated on downstream tasks like segmentation, patch classification, and multiple instance learning, demonstrating minimal performance degradation with AE-compressed reconstructions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of repurposing AEs from LDMs for pathology image compression is somewhat novel. While learned image compression is not entirely new, the specific application to pathology with AEs pre-trained on general web images and the use of pathology-specific fine-tuning metrics adds a distinct flavor. The K-means quantization for the latent space is also a practical contribution. However, it's crucial to note that AEs had been studied previously in histopathology before this paper.

*   **Significance:**  The significance stems from the practical challenge of storing and processing massive pathology image datasets.  The paper addresses a real bottleneck in the field, proposing a solution that balances compression rate and preservation of critical information. The validation on multiple downstream tasks is a key strength, demonstrating the usability of the compressed images for AI-driven analysis. The use of foundation models for evaluating reconstruction quality is a strong point, moving beyond traditional image quality metrics like SSIM and PSNR, which are known to be poorly correlated to the quality of pathology images.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Relevance:** Directly tackles a significant issue in digital pathology.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  Benchmarks multiple AE architectures and compares them to JPEG.
    *   **Downstream Task Validation:**  Demonstrates the effectiveness of the compression method for practical AI applications.
    *   **Pathology-Specific Fine-Tuning:** Adapts general AEs to a specialized domain using relevant loss functions.
    *   **Latent Space Quantization:** Efficient compression while maintaining acceptable performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Contribution:** While the combination of techniques is novel, the individual components (AEs, K-means) are well-established. The use of LDM autoencoders is well used, but not particularly insightful in this context. The paper is not fundamentally changing our understanding of image compression.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While compression rates are important, the paper does not give adequate consideration to the computational resources required to compress and decompress. In many use cases the latency associated with this may be too high.
    *   **Comparison to other Autoencoders** Although the method uses pretrained AEs from LDMs, a rigorous comparison against an autoencoder developed specifically for pathology imaging would provide a more concrete measure of improvement against the current state of the art.
    *   **Decompression Speed:** The paper correctly identifies this as a limitation of the work, but this is a critical issue for clinical adoption. Many pathology use-cases require fast read times, and this may limit the impact of this paper.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper can influence the adoption of learned image compression in pathology, potentially enabling more efficient storage and processing pipelines. It can also encourage the use of foundation models for evaluating image compression algorithms.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a valuable contribution to the field, tackling a practical problem with a well-engineered solution. The systematic evaluation and focus on downstream task performance are particularly strong. However, the novelty of the approach is somewhat limited, and the paper should have better compared with previous autoencoders developed for pathology. In addition, the computational complexity and decompression speed limitations somewhat detract from the overall merit. Therefore, a score of 6.5 is appropriate.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 6/10

### ASMA-Tune: Unlocking LLMs' Assembly Code Comprehension via Structural-Semantic Instruction Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11617v1)
- **Authors**: Xinyi Wang, Jiashui Wang, Peng Chen, Jinbo Su, Yanming Liu, Long Liu, Yangdong Wang, Qiyuan Chen, Kai Yun, Chunfu Jia
- **Abstract**: Analysis and comprehension of assembly code are crucial in various applications, such as reverse engineering. However, the low information density and lack of explicit syntactic structures in assembly code pose significant challenges. Pioneering approaches with masked language modeling (MLM)-based methods have been limited by facilitating natural language interaction. While recent methods based on decoder-focused large language models (LLMs) have significantly enhanced semantic representation, they still struggle to capture the nuanced and sparse semantics in assembly code. In this paper, we propose Assembly Augmented Tuning (ASMA-Tune), an end-to-end structural-semantic instruction-tuning framework. Our approach synergizes encoder architectures with decoder-based LLMs through projector modules to enable comprehensive code understanding. Experiments show that ASMA-Tune outperforms existing benchmarks, significantly enhancing assembly code comprehension and instruction-following abilities. Our model and dataset are public at https://github.com/wxy3596/ASMA-Tune.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the ASMA-Tune paper based on the provided text.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ASMA-Tune, a novel end-to-end instruction tuning framework designed to enhance large language models' (LLMs) understanding and analysis of assembly code. The framework addresses the limitations of existing approaches: MLM-based methods lack generation capabilities, while decoder-focused LLMs struggle with the low-information density and sparse semantics of assembly code. ASMA-Tune synergizes an assembly-specific encoder with a decoder-based LLM via a projector module. The encoder captures hardware-level structural patterns, the projector aligns assembly embeddings with the LLM's semantic space, and the LLM handles natural language interaction. The authors demonstrate the framework's effectiveness through experiments on binary code similarity detection (BCSD) and a new assembly-language instruction-following benchmark (ASMA-Bench), showing state-of-the-art performance compared to existing models, including GPT-4-Turbo and code-instruct LLMs like DeepSeek-Coder. The paper also includes ablation studies that quantify the contributions of each framework component and a detailed dataset analysis. The authors open-source their toolkit and instruction tuning dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses an important and challenging problem: the analysis and comprehension of assembly code by LLMs. While LLMs have shown promise in various domains, their application to assembly code has been hampered by the unique characteristics of this low-level language.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** ASMA-Tune presents a genuinely novel approach to assembly code understanding by decoupling assembly processing through an assembly-specific encoder and then integrating with the generative capabilities of a decoder-based LLM. The projector module's role in aligning assembly embeddings with the LLM's semantic space is a key contribution.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper offers a strong and thorough evaluation. It uses established benchmarks (BCSD) and a new benchmark (ASMA-Bench), provides ablation studies, and includes human assessments. The comparison against strong baselines like GPT-4-Turbo and other code-instruct LLMs lends credibility to the results.
*   **ASMA-Bench Contribution:** The creation and open-sourcing of ASMA-Bench are significant. It provides a valuable resource for the community to further research in this area. The detailed description of how the dataset was generated is important for reproducibility and future development.
*   **Ablation Studies & Analysis**: The ablation studies (dataset, model size, components) give insight on the effect of each component for the final results.
*   **Practical Benefit:** Assembly code analysis is crucial for many real-world applications (reverse engineering, security, optimization). Improving LLMs' capabilities in this area could have significant practical impact.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the problem, the proposed solution, and the experimental setup.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Limitations:** The authors acknowledge that their dataset is limited to the x86-64 architecture. This limits the generalizability of the approach.  While the approach is novel, it's still dependent on a specific architecture and would need adaptation for others.
*   **Metric focus**: While the paper performs ablations and evaluations, the choice of metrics focuses solely on recall, and the average for the BCSD. This limits insight on the impact to precision and F1 score.
*   **Limited Scope**: While BCSD and instruction following are relevant tasks, the paper does not evaluate performance in other potentially important areas, such as vulnerability detection or code optimization. Testing in these specialized domains would strengthen the claim of general assembly code understanding.
*   **Reliance on GPT-4-Turbo for Data Generation:** The approach relies heavily on GPT-4-Turbo for generating training data. This raises questions about potential biases introduced by the LLM. How diverse is the data it produced?

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies in the specific architectural combination of an assembly-specific encoder, a projector module, and a large language model for assembly code understanding. While components of this architecture have been used in other contexts, the synergistic combination within the ASMA-Tune framework is novel.

The significance is in demonstrating a substantial improvement in assembly code comprehension and instruction following compared to existing LLM-based methods. The ASMA-Bench dataset further contributes to the field by providing a resource for future research and development.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of 7.5. Here's why:

*   **The good:** ASMA-Tune is a significant step forward in assembly code understanding by LLMs, addressing a key limitation of existing approaches with a clever and well-executed architecture. The evaluation is strong, and the open-sourcing of code and data is commendable.
*   **The not so good:** The limited dataset architecture, GPT-4 dependency and limited scope of the evaluation hold it back from a higher score. The paper could have explored metrics that show the precision and F1 scores, not only the recall score. Further testing on different architectures could greatly improve the score of the paper. The reliance on GPT-4 Turbo for the data introduces some data biases based on its design and limitations.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10
